They are called emollient, however, even if in other cases they deviate slightly 947 toward drying and moistening, provided only that they possess the capacity to melt the underlying substance of the parts they touch—which happens, evidently, because their heat is moderate. And that after the hardness has been melted there is need of discutient drugs has been stated both in the fifth book of On the Power of Simple Drugs and in the fourteenth of the Therapeutic Method. The best treatment, then, of parts that are hardening—which indeed become scirrhous if they are not treated by medicine according to method—comes about when the one treating considers how far by nature the substance of the body under treatment departs from the commensurate moisture and dryness, as also the constitution of the affection itself, to what degree it has increased, and in addition to these the more and the less in the softening drugs themselves; for if you look to these things, the first use of the emollient drug will be conjectural, yet with art. But drugs are also called emollient even if in other cases they deviate 947 slightly towards drying or moistening, provided only that they possess the property of melting the underlying substance of the parts they touch. This evidently comes about because their heat is moderate. That after the hardness has been melted there is need of discutient drugs has been stated both in the fifth book of On the Power of Simple Drugs and in the fourteenth of the Therapeutic Method. The best treatment, then, of parts that are becoming hardened, which indeed also become scirrhous if they are not treated by medicine according to method, comes about when the practitioner considers how far the substance of the body under treatment has advanced in its nature beyond the well-proportioned moisture and dryness, and likewise the constitution of the affection itself, to what extent it has increased, and in addition to these, the more and less in the softening drugs themselves. ἡμέρας εἴτ’ ἐπιτεῖναι προσῆκεν εἴτ’ ἐκλῦσαι τὴν δύναμιν τοῦ φαρμάκου νοήσεις, ὡς ἐπί τε τῶν ἔμπροσθεν εἴρηται καὶ 948 κατὰ τὴν θεραπευτικὴν μέθοδον. οἱ μὲν οὖν ἰατροὶ συνέθεσαν φάρμακα μαλακτικὰ καὶ διαφορητικὰ ὡς πρὸς ἀμφοτέρους ἀποβλεπόντει τοὺς σκοποὺς, τόν τε τῆς χύσεως τοῦ ἐσκληρυσμένου καὶ σκιῤῥουμένου μορίου, τόν τε τῆς διαφορήσεως, οὐκ ἐναλλὰξ ἑκατέρῳ χρώμενοι, καθάπερ ἐμὲ πράττοντα βλέπετε πολλάκις, ἀλλ’ ἀεὶ τὰ δύο διὰ τοῦ συνθέτου φαρμάκου ποιοῦντα. εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι κατὰ τὴν μίξιν ἤτοι τὰ μαλάττοντα πλέονά ἐστιν ἢ τὰ διαφοροῦντα, καὶ τό γε μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἐν αὐτοῖς οὐκ ὀλίγον. εὔδηλον δὲ ὅτι πολλάκις ἴσα ταῖς δυνάμεσι μίγνυνται, καὶ χρὴ καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων φαρμάκων, οὕτως κᾀπὶ τούτων παρασκευάσασθαι τῷ βουλομένῳ καλῶς ἰατρεύειν πλείω φάρμακα τῶν ὁμογενῶν, ἢ πάντως γε δύο μίγνυσθαι, τό τε ἰσχυρότατον ἐν τῷ γένει καὶ τὸ μετριώτατον, ἵνα ἐκ τῆς διαφόρου μίξεως αὐτῶν ἐπικαίρου γενομένης ἐργάζηται τὰ μεταξὺ τῶν ἄκρων πάμπολλα φάρμακα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποβλεπόντει, ποιοῦντα, ἐπικαίρου
days, you will understand whether it is fitting to intensify or to relax the power of the drug, as has been said both in the preceding books and 948 in the Therapeutic Method. Now the physicians have compounded emollient and discutient drugs as looking to both aims at once, that of the liquefaction of the hardened and scirrhous part and that of discussion, not using each of the two alternately, as you often see me doing, but always effecting the two things through the compound drug. It is quite evident that in the mixture either the softening ingredients are more than the discutient ones, or the reverse, and the degree of more and less among them is no small matter. It is quite evident too that often they are mixed in equal proportions as regards their powers; and it is necessary, just as in the case of the other drugs, so also in the case of these, for one who wishes to practise medicine well to prepare for himself several drugs of the same kind, or at any rate to have two to mix, the strongest in the kind and the most moderate, in order that from their varied mixture, made in a manner suited to the occasion, he may produce the very many drugs that lie between the extremes.

…days you will perceive whether it is fitting to intensify or to slacken the potency of the drug, as has been said both in the preceding cases and 948 in the Therapeutic Method. Now the physicians have compounded emollient and discutient drugs with a view to both aims together: that of the liquefaction of the hardened and scirrhous part, and that of discussion. They do not use each in alternation, as you often see me doing; they always accomplish the two by means of the compound drug. It is quite clear that in the mixture either the emollient ingredients are the more numerous or the discutient ones, and the more and less among them is no small matter. It is quite clear too that they are often mixed in equal potencies. Just as with the other drugs, so with these, the one who wishes to treat well must have prepared several drugs of the same kind, or at any rate two to be mixed, the strongest in the kind and the most moderate, so that from their differing mixture, made at the opportune moment, he may produce a great many drugs between the extremes.

Greek (Kühn)
οὐ γὰρ τὴν αὐτὴν ἔχει δύναμιν τὸ στέαρ βδελλίῳ τε καὶ στύρακι καὶ ἀμμωνιακῷ θυμιάματι, καθάπερ οὐδὲ αὐτῶν τῶν στεάτων τὸ χήνειόν τε 949 καὶ ὀρνίθειον ἢ παρδάλειον ἢ λεόντειον ἡ ὑαίνης ἤ τινος ἄλλου ζώου. κατὰ γὰρ τὴν κρᾶσιν τοῦ ζώου καὶ τὸ στέαρ ἐστι, τῶν μὲν θερμοτέρων θερμαντικώτερον, τῶν δὲ ξηροτέρων ξηραντικώτερον, καὶ γινώσκουσί γε οἱ πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν ἰσχυρότατον μὲν εἶναι κατ’ ἄμφω, λέγω δὴ τὸ θερμαίνειν τε καὶ διαφορεῖν, τὸ τοῦ λέοντος στέαρ. ἐγγὺς δὲ αὐτῷ τὸ τῶν παρδάλεων, ὡς ὀλίγον ἔχειν τὸ μαλακτικόν. ἥττονα δὲ ἐν τῷ θερμαίνειν τε καὶ διαφορεῖν ἔχει τὸ χήνειον τὴν δύναμιν, ἔχει δέ τι πλέον ἐν τῷ μαλάττειν. ἀνάλογον δὲ ἀφέστηκε τοῦδε καὶ τὸ τῶν ἀλεκτορίδων. τούτοις μὲν οὖν ἅπασι καὶ τὸ λεπτομέρεστερον εἶναι τῶν ἐν τοῖς τετράποσι ζώοις ὑπάρχει. διαφορὰ δὲ πάλιν ἐν ἐκείνοις ἐστὶ παμπόλλη, τοῦ μὲν ὑείου μαλακωτάτου τε καὶ ὑγροτάτου καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἀσθενεστάτου τῶν ἄλλων ὄντος, ὡς ἁρμόττειν ἐπὶ σωμάτων μὲν ἁπαλῶν, οἷα τὰ τῶν γυναικῶν ἐστι καὶ παίδων καὶ εὐνούχων καὶ τῶν ἁβροδιαίτων ἀνδρῶν ἐν σκιατροφίᾳ καὶ ἀγυμνασίᾳ καὶ λουτροῖς πλείοσι ποτίμων ὑδάτων ἐκτεθηλυσμένα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἡ ὑαίνης, ἀμμωνιακῷ θυμιάματι
For fat does not have the same power as bdellium and storax and ammoniac incense, just as, among the fats themselves, neither do the goose 949 and the hen's, or leopard's or lion's or hyena's, or that of some other animal. For the fat too is in accordance with the temperament of the animal: that of the hotter animals is more heating, that of the drier more drying; and most physicians know that the strongest in both respects, I mean in heating and in dispersing, is the fat of the lion. Close to it is that of leopards, so that it has little of the emollient. Goose fat has a lesser power in heating and dispersing, but it has something more in softening. And that of hens stands at a proportionate distance from this one. To all of these, moreover, belongs also being finer-parted than those in four-footed animals. But again among those there is a very great difference, pig's fat being the softest and moistest and for this reason the weakest of the others, so that it suits tender bodies, such as those of women and children and eunuchs and of delicately-living men, bodies made effeminate by rearing in the shade, by lack of exercise, and by rather frequent baths of fresh waters.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀρνίθειον, ἡ ὑαίνης, λεπτομέρεστερον, ἀμμωνιακῷ θυμιάματι, ἀνάλογον δὲ ἀφέστηκε τοῦδε
For fat does not have the same power as bdellium, storax and ammoniac incense, just as among the fats themselves goose fat and 949 fowl's fat do not have the same power as that of the leopard or the lion or the hyena or of any other animal. For the fat too is according to the temperament (krasis) of the animal: that of the hotter animals is more heating, that of the drier more drying. And indeed most physicians know that the strongest in both respects, I mean heating and dispersing, is the fat of the lion. Close to it is that of leopards, so that it has little emollient quality. Goose fat has a weaker power in heating and dispersing, but it has somewhat more in softening. And that of hens stands at a proportionate remove from this one. Now all of these also have the property of being finer-particled than those in four-footed animals. Among those, in turn, there is a very great difference: pig's fat is the softest and moistest and for this reason the weakest of the others, so that it suits soft bodies, such as are those of women and children and eunuchs and of men of delicate living, made effeminate by rearing in the shade, lack of exercise, and frequent baths of drinkable water.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But that of goats and of cattle, to the extent that it is stronger than that of swine, to that same extent is suited to bodies 950 that are harder, both by nature, and to the more scirrhous among the harder ones. That of bulls and he-goats is suited to the stronger bodies and affections. And the same difference relative to one another, according to the more and the less, holds also for the others. I shall begin from the weaker ones, having first spoken beforehand about the affection that consists in hardness and scirrhosis. For since the resistance under the pressing of the fingers is an accident common to parts that are inflamed and to those that are being hardened, the affections are distinguished by the painful and the painless, for this is no longer common to both.

That of goats and oxen, by as much as it is stronger than that of swine, by so much is it suited to bodies 950 that are harder, also by nature, and to the more scirrhous among the harder [conditions]. I shall begin from the weaker ones, having first spoken about the affection that consists in hardness and scirrhosis. For since the resistance to the pressure of the fingers is an attribute common to parts that are inflamed and to parts that are hardened, the affections are distinguished by the painful and the painless; for this is no longer common to both.

ὥσπερ δὲ ἐν τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασιν ὅσα μεταπίπτειν εἰς ἄλληλα πέφυκεν, ἔνεστι μεθόριον ἀμφοτέρων ἠρέμα μέτοχον, οὕτως καὶ τῶν φλεγμονωδῶν ὄγκων πρὸς τοὺς ἄνευ φλεγμονῆς σκίῤῥους, οἷοί περ οἱ τῶν ἐσκληρυσμένων εἰσὶν, ὑπάρχει τις ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ διάθεσις, ἀνώδυνος μὲν, εἰ μὴ τοὺς δακτύλους ἐπερείδοις, ὀδυνωμένη δὲ τοῦτο πραττόντων, ἣν σαφοῦς τε καὶ συντόμου διδασκαλίας ἕνεκα ἤτοι φλεγμονὴν σκιῤῥώδη λεκτέον ἢ σκίῤῥον φλεγμονώδη, μὴ προσέχοντας ἐνταῦθα τῷ 951 κατὰ τὸν ὄγκον μεγέθει. τῆς γὰρ ἰδέας τῶν παθῶν ἐστιν, οὐ τοῦ καθ’ ἕτερον αὐτῶν ποσοῦ φλεγ μονὴ καὶ σκιῤῥος ὀνόματα, μεγάλης μὲν φλεγμονῆς γενομένης ἢ διὰ τὸ τοῦ φλεγμαίνοντος μορίου μέγεθος ἢ διὰ τὴν τοῦ παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκου, μικρᾶς δὲ τῆς ἤτοι μικρὸν τόπον κατειληφυίας ἢ βραχὺν ὄγκον ἐχούσης. εἰρήσεται δὲ νῦν ἐμοὶ μικρᾶς φλεγμονῆς καὶ μικροῦ σκίῤῥου θεραπεία, καθάπερ καὶ μεγάλης τε καὶ μεγάλου, κατὰ τὰς τῶν παθῶν ἰδέας. νοηθήσεται γὰρ ἐκ τούτων καὶ τῶν ἑτέρων λεγομένων μεγάλων τε καὶ μικρῶν ἡ θεραπεία. φλεγμονῆς μὲν οὖν μεγάλης διὰ καταπλασμάτων ἴασις γίνεται καί τινων ὑγρῶν φαρμάκων. τῆς δὲ μικρᾶς, ὅταν σκιῤῥώδει σκληρότητι μιχθῇ, καὶ διὰ καταπλασμάτων μὲν ἔστιν ὅτε, καὶ διὰ φαρμάκων δὲ κηρωτοειδῶν τε καὶ ὑγρῶν, ἔτι τε τῶν μαλακτικῶν ἐμπλάστρων. διὰ τοιούτων δὲ καὶ τῆς μικρᾶς σκληρότητος ἡ ἴασις γίνεται, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ ταύτης μὲν ἁπλοῦς ὁ σκοπὸς, ὥσπερ καὶ τῆς φλεγμονῆς συνισταμένης. ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς ἐχούσης φλεγμονώδους τι σκληρότητος σύνθετος καὶ ὁ σκοπὸς γίγνεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συνισταμένης
Just as in all other things that are naturally such as to change into one another there is a borderland partaking slightly of both, so too between the inflammatory swellings and the scirrhi without inflammation, such as those of indurated parts are, there exists a certain intermediate condition, painless, unless you press the fingers upon it, but painful when one does this; which, for the sake of clear and concise teaching, must be called either scirrhous inflammation or inflammatory scirrhus, not attending here to the 951 magnitude of the swelling. For 'inflammation' and 'scirrhus' are names of the form of the affections, not of the quantity in either of them, an inflammation being large either on account of the size of the inflamed part or on account of that of the swelling contrary to nature, and small when it has either occupied a small place or has a slight swelling. I shall now speak of the treatment of a small inflammation and of a small scirrhus, just as also of a large inflammation and a large scirrhus, according to the forms of the affections. For from these the treatment of those called large and small in the other sense will also be understood. The cure of a large inflammation, then, comes about through cataplasms and certain liquid drugs. That of a small one, when it is mixed with a scirrhous hardness, comes about sometimes through cataplasms, and also through drugs that are cerate-like and liquid, and further through the emollient plasters. Through such means the cure of a small hardness also comes about; but in the case of this the aim is simple, just as also when inflammation alone is present. In the case of a hardness that has something inflammatory, however, the aim too becomes composite.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἠρέμα μέτοχον, τὴν τοῦ παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκου (μέγεθος understood), φλεγ μονὴ (word split in Kühn), συνισταμένης
Just as in all other things whose nature is to pass over into one another there is a borderland between the two that shares moderately in both, so also between phlegmonous swellings and the scirrhi without phlegmone, such as are those of parts that have become hardened, there exists a certain intermediate condition. It is painless unless you press the fingers on it, but painful when people do so. For the sake of clear and concise teaching it must be called either a scirrhous phlegmone or a phlegmonous scirrhus, without attending here to the 951 magnitude of the swelling. For 'phlegmone' and 'scirrhus' are names of the form of the affections, not of the quantity in either of them. A phlegmone is large either because of the magnitude of the part affected by phlegmone or because of that of the swelling contrary to nature, and small when it has either occupied a small place or has a slight swelling. I shall now speak of the treatment of a small phlegmone and a small scirrhus, as also of a large one of each, according to the forms of the affections. For from these the treatment of the other things called large and small will also be understood. The cure of a large phlegmone, then, comes about through poultices and certain liquid drugs. The cure of a small one, when it has been mixed with scirrhous hardness, comes about sometimes through poultices, but also through cerate-like and liquid drugs, and further through the emollient plasters. Through such means the cure of a small hardness also comes about. In its case the aim is simple, as also when a phlegmone is forming. But in the case of a hardness that has something phlegmonous in it, the aim too becomes compound.

Greek (Kühn)
[Περὶ τῆς παρύγρου.] Καὶ διὰ τοῦτο 952 καὶ φάρμακον σύνθετόν πώς ἐστιν, οἷον καὶ τοῦτο τὸ πᾶσι γινωσκόμενον, ἐπῃνημένον δὲ μεγάλως ὑπ’ ἄλλων τέ τινων καὶ μάλιστα τοῦ Ἥρα, τὸ καλούμενον πάρυγρον. ἰᾶται γὰρ τοῦτο βραχεῖαν ἔτι φλεγμονὴν ἔχον τὸ μόριον, οὐ μετὰ πολὺν χρόνον τῆς σκευασίας, ἀλλ’ εὐθέως ἐπιτιθέμενον. σκληρότητα δὲ ἀνώδυνον ἐπικτωμένου τοῦ πεπονθότος μορίου, κᾂν ἔτι βραχεῖ χρονιώτερον ᾖ τοῦ προσφάτου, χρήσιμον ὑπάρχει. καθάπερ γε καὶ ἂν ἀπηλλαγμένον παντάπασι τὸ πεπονθὸς μόριον τῆς φλεγμονώδους διαθέσεως, ἄμεινον ἐπ’ αὐτῷ τὸ χρονιώτερον φάρμακον. ἀνάλογον δὲ δηλονότι τῇ τῆς σκληρότητος ἐπιτάσει συναύξεσθαι τὸν χρόνον προσήκει τοῦ φαρμάκου, τὸ μέντοι λίαν ἐσκληρυσμένον μόριον οὐδὲ τὸ χρόνιον μαλάξει φάρμακον. μικτὴν γὰρ ἐπαγγελίαν τε καὶ δύναμιν ἔχει τοῦτο δὴ τὸ πάρυγρον ὀνομαζόμενον, ὅτι καὶ τὴν ὕλην μικτήν. εἴ γε τὸ μὲν ὕειον στέαρ καὶ ὁ κηρὸς ἀσθενέστατα τῶν μαλακτικῶν ἐστι, λιθάργυρος δὲ καὶ ψιμύθιον οὐδὲν ἔχει μαλακτικὸν, ὡς ἐμάθομεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
[On the parhygros.] And for this reason 952 the drug too is in a way composite, such as this one which is known to everyone and greatly praised by certain others and especially by Heras, the one called parhygron (moist). For this cures a part that still has a slight inflammation when it is applied not a long time after its preparation but immediately. But when the affected part has acquired in addition a painless hardness, even if the drug is still a little longer standing than the fresh one, it is useful; just as, if the affected part has been altogether freed from the inflammatory condition, the drug of longer standing is better for it. And clearly the age of the drug ought to be increased in proportion to the intensity of the hardness; however, a part that has become excessively hardened not even the aged drug will soften. For this drug called parhygron has a mixed profession and a mixed power, because its material too is mixed: since pig's fat and wax are the weakest of emollients, while litharge and white lead have nothing emollient, as we have learned.

[On the parhygros.] And for this reason 952 the drug too is in a way compound, such as this one known to everyone and highly praised by certain others and above all by Heras, the so-called parhygron. For this heals a part that still has slight inflammation, when it is applied not long after its preparation but immediately. But when the affected part acquires a painless hardness, it is useful even if it is a little older than the freshly prepared. Just so, if the affected part has been entirely freed from the inflammatory condition, the older drug is better for it. Clearly the age of the drug ought to increase in proportion to the intensification of the hardness; a part that is excessively hardened, however, not even the aged drug will soften. For this so-called parhygron has a mixed promise and power, because it also has mixed material. Pig's fat and wax are the weakest of the emollients, while litharge and white lead have nothing emollient, as we have learned.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀτονώτερα, ἐξινισμένου
Heras, who praised this drug most of all, compounds 953 it from the following ingredients, in these very words: Take fresh pig's fat with the fibres removed, 44 ounces; wax, 24 ounces; white lead, 6 ounces; litharge, 6 ounces. The meltable ingredients are poured over the dry ones, and it sets with time. Just as I said that wax and pig's fat are of a weak emollient power, so too, if you mix a little wax into oil, you will make what is called by physicians cerelaeum (wax-oil). The oil, evidently, must be neither that called omphacine and omotribes (of unripe olives), nor that which has had olive shoots added to it in its preparation. [The differences and powers of the emollient drugs by kind.] Now the emollients that are thus weak also treat small inflammations; those weaker than these (ἀτονώτερα), the slight hardnesses without inflammation.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξινισμένου, ἀτονώτερα, θαλλοὺς ἐλαιῶν
Heras, who praised this drug most of all, composes 953 it from the following ingredients, in these words: ℞ fresh pig's fat, with the fibres removed, 44 ounces; wax, 24 ounces; white lead, 6 ounces; litharge, 6 ounces. Pour the meltable ingredients over the dry ones; it sets with time. Just as I said that wax and pig's fat are of weak emollient power, so too, if you mix a little wax with oil, you will make what physicians call cerelaeum (wax-oil). The oil, clearly, must be neither the so-called omphacine and omotribes oil (oil of unripe olives), nor oil that has taken up olive shoots in its preparation. [The differences by kind, and the powers, of emollient drugs.] Now, emollients that are weak in this way also treat small inflammations; those that are feebler than these treat slight hardnesses without inflammation.

Greek (Kühn)
τὰ δ’ ἔτι τούτων ἰσχυρότερα τὰς μείζους, ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ τῆς ἐγγὺς ἡκούσης σκίῤῥου σκληρότητος ἰατικὰ, δραστικώτατά ἐστιν, ὁποῖον ὑπάρχει τό τε τῶν μυελῶν γένος ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἀλλήλων διαφερόντων, ὡς ἐφεξῆς διαιρήσω, 954 τό τε τῶν βδελλίων ἀμφοῖν, Ἀραβικοῦ τε καὶ Σκυθικοῦ, τό τε τῶν στυράκων. καὶ γὰρ καὶ τούτων δραστικώτατος ὁ κατὰ τὴν χρόαν ὠχρὸς, ὅσπερ καὶ κάλλιστος ἐν Παμφυλίᾳ γεννᾶται. ὁ δὲ ἐπὶ τὸ φαιότερον ἀποκεχωρηκὼς τοῦδε πολλαχόθι τε γίνεται καὶ τῇ δυνάμει πολὺ τοῦ κατὰ τὸν ὠχρὸν ἀπολείπεται. ῥέπει δ’ οὖν καὶ οὗτος αὐτὸς ἐν τῷ χρονίζειν ἐπὶ τὸ χρυσίζον χρῶμα. τούτῳ μὶν οὖν εἰς τὰς ἀντιδότους χρώμεθα διὰ τὸ σπανίζειν, ὥστε αὐτοκράτορί γε σκευάζων ὁτιοῦν διὰ στύρακος ἀεὶ τούτῳ χρήσῃ. κᾂν τῶν πλησίον τις ᾖ μεγαλόφρων εἰς δαπάνην φαρμάκων, κᾀκείνῳ συνθήσεις ἐκ τοιούτου στύρακος τὸ φάρμακον. ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων δὲ τοῦ φαιοτέρου τὸν ἄριστον ἐκλέξῃ, τῇ τε ὀσμῇ κρίνων μάλιστα καὶ τῇ χρόᾳ, φαυλότερος γὰρ ὁ μελάντερος. ἔστι δὲ τῶν ἰσχυρῶς μαλαττόντων τὰ σκιῤῥούμενα, τὸ καλούμενον ἀμμωνιακὸν θυμίαμα καὶ μᾶλλον αὐτοῦ τὸ λιπαρόν τε καὶ νέον. τούτων δὲ ἧττον μὲν, ἀλλὰ καὶ αὐτὰ δραστήρια αἴγειόν τε καὶ βόειον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὗτος αὐτὸς, μᾶλλον αὐτοῦ
Those still stronger than these [treat] the greater [hardnesses], just as those that are curative of a hardness that has come near to scirrhus are the most effective, of which sort is the kind of the marrows, differing from one another in more and less, as I shall distinguish next, 954 and that of both bdelliums, the Arabian and the Scythian, and that of the storaxes. For of these too the most effective is the one pale yellow in colour, which indeed is produced at its finest in Pamphylia. The one that has departed from this toward the greyer is produced in many places and falls far short in power of the pale yellow one. And indeed this one itself, too, inclines in the course of time toward a golden colour. This one, then, we use for the antidotes, because it is scarce, so that when preparing anything with storax for the emperor at any rate you will always use this one. And if any of those near at hand is liberal with regard to expenditure on drugs, for him too you will compound the drug from such storax. In the case of the others, you will select the best of the greyer sort, judging chiefly by the smell and by the colour, for the blacker is inferior. Among those that strongly soften parts that are becoming scirrhous is the so-called ammoniac incense, and more so the oily and fresh sort of it. Less than these, but nevertheless themselves active, are goat and ox [fat].
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μὶν, τῶν πλησίον, τούτῳ μὶν οὖν εἰς τὰς ἀντιδότους χρώμεθα, ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων
Those that are stronger still than these treat the greater ones, just as those that cure a hardness which has come close to a scirrhus are the most drastic; of this sort is the class of the marrows, which differ from one another by more and less, as I shall distinguish next, 954 and that of both bdelliums, the Arabian and the Scythian, and that of the storaxes. For of these too the most drastic is the one pale yellow in colour, which is also produced finest in Pamphylia. The one that has departed from this towards the duskier is produced in many places and falls far short in power of the pale one. Yet this very one too, as it ages, inclines towards a golden colour. This one, then, we use for antidotes on account of its scarcity, so that when preparing anything with storax for an emperor you will always use this. And if any of those near you is lavish in expenditure on drugs, for him too you will compound the drug from storax of this kind. For the others, you will select the best of the duskier, judging chiefly by its smell and its colour; for the blacker is inferior. Among the things that strongly soften parts that are becoming scirrhous is the so-called ammoniacum incense, and more than it the kind that is fatty and fresh. Less than these, but themselves also active, are goat's and ox fat.

Among these themselves, the fat of he-goats and bulls is stronger, while that of cows and she-goats is weaker than these, yet not ineffective 955 either. That of kids and calves is weaker still even than these, but is nevertheless more active than that of the young pig. A power more discutient than these, though not more emollient, is possessed by lion's and leopard's fat, and that of the hyena and of the goose, inasmuch as they also have a substance of finer parts than goat's and ox's fat. That of hens is ranked in between goose fat and pig's fat. Just as also, among the marrows, the best is that of calves. And resins, all those which do not have a pungent smell, when mixed with any of the aforementioned substances, provide the adhesive quality to the compounded drugs and do not destroy their emollient quality; most suitable are the so-called Chian and the larch resin. After these comes the peuke-pine resin, and after that the fir resin. The strobilos (pine-cone) resin is strongly pungent, while the pine resin (pityine) is non-oily and greatly drying; they call it specifically physema. These, then, are to be avoided. The Chian and the fir resin are to be used not by themselves alone, but together with wax and some one of the other substances, whatever seems to suit the condition. And Bruttian pitch too has the same power, as having something adhesive and oily in it.

And further, butter too is of the same order, 957 as are the decoction of marsh-mallow and that of fenugreek. [Αἱ τῶν μαλακτικῶν φαρμάκων κατ’ εἴδη διαφοραὶ καὶ δυνάμεις.] Ἐπεὶ δὲ οὐ πάντες εἰσὶ τοιοῦτοι καὶ πρόκειται κατὰ τήνδε τὴν πραγματείαν, οὐχ ὥσπερ ἐν τῇ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου, μόνην εἰπεῖν τὴν μέθοδον, ἀλλ’ ἅμα παραδείγματι τῶν κατὰ μέρος ἑνί τε καὶ δευτέρῳ, προστεθῆναι δεῖ καὶ τὰ πείρᾳ προκείμενα φάρμακα, ἃ πρόσκειται τοῖς πρὸ ἡμῶν ἐν τῷ φαρμακευτικῷ μέρει τῆς τέχνης ἐνδόξοις, ἐπὶ τὰς κατ’ εἴδη διαφορὰς ἐπὶ τῶν μαλακτικῶν φαρμάκων ἀφίξομαι τὴν ἀρχὴν ἀπὸ τῶν μαλακωτάτων ποιησάμενος 959 αὐτοῖς, ἃ καὶ τὰς μικρὰς ἰᾶται φλεγμονάς. τῶν συνθέτων μαλακτικῶν τὰ μέν ἐστιν, ὡς ἂν εἴποι τις, ἀσθενέστερα κατὰ τὴν πρώτην τάξιν. ἔνια δὲ οὐ πολλῷ τούτων ἰσχυρότερα κατὰ δευτέραν, καί τινα κατὰ γ΄. ἕτερα. καὶ πρὸς αὐτοῖς κατὰ δ΄. ἄλλα. χρῆσθαι δὲ προσῆκεν, ὡς προείρηται, πρὸς μὲν τὰς βραχείας σκληρότητας καὶ τὰ μαλακὰ σώματα τοῖς τῆς πρώτης τάξεως. πρὸς δὲ τὰ τούτων ἰσχυρότερα σώματά τε καὶ πάθη τοῖς τῆς δευτέρας, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τοῖς τῆς τρίτης καὶ τῆς τετάρτης ἀνάλογον. ὑπογράφουσι δὲ καὶ τὰ καθ’ ἑκάστην αὐτῶν ἰδίᾳ. [Μαλακτικὰ φάρμακα πρώτης τάξεως.] Βούτυρον ἢ ῥητίνη ἡ Κολοφωνία καὶ κηρὸς λευκὸς, μὴ παλαιὸς, ἴσα τῷ σταθμῷ τὰ γ΄. τηκόμενα μετ’ ἀλλήλων, μαλακτικὰ πρώτης τάξεώς ἐστι. καὶ ἡ πάρυγρος, ὅταν ᾖ πρόσφατος, ἡ χρονισθεῖσα δὲ διαφορητικωτέρα γίνεται, ἀσθενεστάτη δὲ τῶν μαλακτικῶν, κηρὸς μετὰ βουτύρου τακεὶς ἢ διὰ σουσίνου ἢ Κομμαγηνοῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πείρᾳ προκείμενα, πρόσκειται, ὑπογράφουσι, ἢ (in Βούτυρον ἢ ῥητίνη), Κομμαγηνοῦ
[The differences and powers of emollient drugs according to kinds.] But since not all are such men, and since it is proposed in this treatise not, as in that of the Therapeutic Method, to state the method alone, but together with one or two examples of the particular cases, there must also be added the drugs that are established by experience, which are set down by those before us who are esteemed in the pharmaceutical part of the art; I shall therefore proceed to the differences according to kinds among the emollient drugs, making my beginning from the softest 959 of them, which cure even small inflammations. Of the compound emollients some are, as one might say, weaker, in the first order; some not much stronger than these, in the second; and certain others in the third; and in addition to these, others in the fourth. It is fitting to use, as has been said before, against slight hardnesses and soft bodies those of the first order; against bodies and affections stronger than these, those of the second, just as also those of the third and the fourth proportionately. And they also describe those falling under each of these separately. [Emollient drugs of the first order.] Butter or Colophonian resin and white wax, not old, the three equal in weight, melted together with one another, are emollients of the first order. So too the moist (parhygros) [cerate], when it is fresh; but one that has aged becomes more discutient. The weakest of the emollients is wax melted with butter, or by means of lily-oil (sousinon) or Commagene [ointment].
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πείρᾳ προκείμενα, πρόσκειται, μαλακωτάτων ... αὐτοῖς, ὑπογράφουσι, Βούτυρον ἢ ῥητίνη (ἢ where the three-ingredient count suggests καί), ἀσθενεστάτη (feminine: may refer back to ἡ πάρυγρος), σουσίνου, Κομμαγηνοῦ
[The differences by species, and the potencies, of emollient drugs.] But since not all are of this kind, and since what is proposed in this treatise is not, as in the Therapeutic Method, to state the method alone, but to state it together with an example of the particulars, one or a second, and the drugs established by experience must also be added, those set down by the men before us who were renowned in the pharmaceutical part of the art, I shall come to the differences by species among the emollient drugs, making my beginning from the mildest of them, which also cure small inflammations. 959 Of the compound emollients, some are, as one might say, weaker, in the first order; some, not much stronger than these, in the second; certain others in the third; and, besides these, others in the fourth. One should use, as has been said before, for slight indurations and for soft bodies those of the first order; for bodies and affections stronger than these, those of the second; and likewise those of the third and of the fourth in proportion. They also describe separately the drugs belonging to each of these orders. [Emollient drugs of the first order.] Butter or Colophonian resin and white wax that is not old, the three in equal weight, melted together, are emollients of the first order. So too is the parhygros, when it is fresh; when it has aged it becomes more discutient. And the weakest of the emollients is wax melted with butter, or with susinum or Commagenum.

καὶ τούτων ἀσθενέστερόν ἐστι τὸ καλούμενον κηρέλαιον, ὅταν εἰς λίτραν ἐλαίου χαλαστικοῦ μηδεμίαν 960 ἔχοντος στύψιν ἐμβληθῶσι κηροῦ οὐγγίαι ιβ΄. ἢ ἑκκαίδεκα. καὶ αἱ διὰ τῶν ὠῶν δὲ κηρωταὶ βραχύ τι χαλαστικὸν ἔχουσι. ἰσχυρότεραι δὲ τούτων, ἀλλὰ ἀσθενεῖς ἔτι καὶ αὗται κηρωταὶ, αἱ δι’ οἰσύπου καὶ ῥητίνης καὶ κηροῦ, τηκομένων ἴσῳ τῷ σταθμῷ. ἐὰν δὲ ᾖ παλαιότερά πως αὐτὰ, δεήσεται μίξεως ἐλαίου τινὸς τῶν χαλαστικῶν ἢ σουσίνου ἢ Κομμαγηνοῦ ἢ στέατος ὑείου ἢ ὀρνιθείου ἢ χηνείου. μαλακτικὰ φάρμακα δευτέρας τάξεως. ὡς ἐν τοῖς ἁπλοῖς φαρμάκοις, ἐποιήσαμεν τέσσαρας τάξεις τῶν ὁμογενῶν, τὸ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἐν αὐτοῖς ὁριζούσας, ἐνίων δ’ ἔφην ἀμφίβολον εἶναι τὴν δύναμιν, εἴτ’ ἐπὶ τελευτῇ τῆς πρώτης τάξεως εἴτ’ ἐν ἀρχῇ τῆς δευτέρας, θετέον ἐστὶ τὴν δύναμιν αὐτῶν, ὡσαύτως καὶ περὶ τῆς δευτέρας τε καὶ τρίτης τῶν τε ταύταις ἐφεξῆς. ἐπ’ ἐνίων δὲ θαῤῥήσας ἀποφαίνεσθαι τινὰ μὲν ἐν ἀρχῇ τῆσδε, τινὰ δὲ ἐπὶ τῇ τελευτῇ τῆσδε τῆς τάξεως ἔφην ὑπάρχειν, ὥσπερ ἔνια χωρὶς προσθήκης ἤτοι τῆς πρώτης ἢ τῆς δευτέρας ἢ τῆς γ΄. ἢ τῆς δ΄. τάξεως ἔφην εἶναι.

And weaker than these is the so-called cerate-oil (kerelaion), when into a litra of relaxing oil having no 960 astringency twelve or sixteen ounces of wax are put. And the cerates made with eggs, too, have some slight relaxing quality. Stronger than these, but still weak themselves, are the cerates made of oesypum, resin and wax, melted together in equal weight. But if they are somewhat older, they will need an admixture of one of the relaxing oils, either susinon or Commagene oil, or of pig's or hen's or goose fat. Emollient drugs of the second order. As in the case of the simple drugs, we made four orders of those of the same kind, defining the more and the less among them, and of some I said that the power is ambiguous, whether their power is to be placed at the end of the first order or at the beginning of the second, and likewise concerning the second and the third and those following upon these. In the case of some, having ventured to make a pronouncement, I said that certain ones are at the beginning of this order and certain ones at the end of this order, just as I said that some are, without any addition, of the first or the second or the third or the fourth order.

And weaker than these is what is called wax-oil (kerelaion), when twelve or sixteen ounces of wax are put into a litra of a relaxing oil that has no 960 astringency. The cerates made with eggs also have a slight relaxing quality. Stronger than these, but these too still weak, are the cerates made of oesypum, resin and wax, melted together in equal weight. If these ingredients are somewhat old, the mixture will need an admixture of one of the relaxing oils, either the lily oil (susinum) or the Commagenian, or of pig's, fowl's or goose fat. Emollient drugs of the second order. As in the case of the simple drugs, we made four orders of things of the same kind, defining the more and the less among them. Of some I said that their power is ambiguous, that is, whether their power is to be placed at the end of the first order or at the beginning of the second, and likewise also concerning the second and third orders and those that follow them. But in some cases I ventured to make a declaration and said that some belong at the beginning of this order and others at the end of that order, just as I said that some, without qualification, belong either to the first, or the second, or the third, or the fourth order.

Greek (Kühn)
πολλῆς δὲ πείρας τῆς κατὰ μέρος ἐπὶ πολλῶν τε σωμάτων καὶ 961 παθῶν αἱ τοιαῦται τῶν ἀποφάσεων δέονται, διὸ κᾂν μετ’ ἐμέ τις ὁμοίως ἐμοὶ φιλόπονός τε καὶ ζηλωτικὸς ἀληθείας γένηται, μὴ προπετῶς ἐκ δυοῖν ἢ τριῶν χρήσεων ἀποφαινέσθω. πολλάκις γὰρ αὐτῷ φανεῖται διὰ τῆς μακρᾶς πείρας, ὥσπερ ἐφάνη κᾀμοὶ, ὅτι τὰ κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τάξιν εἰρημένα εἰς τὴν γ΄. ἀρχομένην μετατεθῆναι δεῖ ἢ κατὰ τὴν ἀρχὴν ἁπλῶς τῆς γ΄. ἅτε γὰρ καὶ τῶν σωμάτων τῆς διαφορᾶς, οἷς προσάγεται τὰ φάρμακα καὶ τῶν παθῶν αὐτῶν οὐκ ὀλίγην ἐχόντων ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον τὴν ὑπεροχὴν, χαλεπώτατόν ἐστιν ἐπὶ πᾶσιν ἀπόφασιν ποιήσασθαι μίαν, ἐξ ἐπιμέτρου δὲ καὶ αὐτῶν τῶν φαρμάκων τῶν ἁπλῶν οὐ μικρὰν ἐχόντων διαφοράν. ἐμοῦ δὲ ἐπὶ τῶν ἀρίστων καθ’ ἕκαστον εἶδός τε καὶ γένος ἀποφηναμένου τὴν δύναμιν, ἐὰν ἐπὶ τοῦ χειρίστου ποιήσηταί τις τήνδε διὰ τῆς πείρας ἐξέτασιν, ποιήσεται μὲν ψευδῶς, οὐ διώρικε δὲ τὰς τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτῶν τάξεις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀρχομένην, ἢ κατὰ τὴν ἀρχὴν ἁπλῶς τῆς γ΄
But such pronouncements require much particular experience, on many bodies and 961 affections; and so, if after me anyone should arise who is, like me, industrious and zealous for truth, let him not make pronouncements rashly on the basis of two or three uses. For it will often appear to him through long experience, as it appeared to me also, that the things stated as belonging to the second rank must be transferred into the third as it begins, or simply at the beginning of the third. For since the difference of the bodies to which the drugs are applied, and of the affections themselves, has no small excess in the more and the less, it is most difficult to make a single pronouncement upon all of them, and in addition the simple drugs themselves have no small difference. And since I have pronounced on the power in the case of the best of each species and kind, if someone makes this examination by experience on the worst, he will make it falsely, and he has not defined the ranks of their power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἅτε γὰρ καὶ τῶν σωμάτων τῆς διαφορᾶς, ὑπεροχὴν, εἰς τὴν γ΄. ἀρχομένην, οὐ διώρικε
Pronouncements of this kind require much particular experience over many bodies and 961 affections. Therefore, even if someone after me should prove, as I am, industrious and zealous for truth, let him not rashly pronounce on the basis of two or three uses. For through long experience it will often appear to him, as it has also appeared to me, that what was stated under the second order must be transferred into the third at its beginning, or simply to the beginning of the third. For since the bodies to which the drugs are applied, and the affections themselves, show no small excess in the more and the less, it is most difficult to make a single pronouncement for all cases; and, in addition, the simple drugs themselves have no small difference among themselves. And since I pronounced on the power in the case of the best of each species and genus, if someone makes this examination through experience on the worst, he will make it falsely, and he has not determined the orders of their power.

Greek (Kühn)
ἀσφαλέστατον οὖν ἐστι, τὴν αὐτῶν τῶν φαρμάκων πρὸς ἄλληλα διαφορὰν ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον 962 ἀκριβῶς γράφειν, ὥσπερ ἐπί τε τῶν ἄλλων ἐποίησα καὶ νῦν ποιήσω. [Διαφόρους γίγνεσθαι διαφορὰς ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον κατὰ τὴν μαλακτικὴν τοῦ Μνασαίου ἔμπλαστρον κατὰ διάφορον τῶν ἁπλῶν ποσότητά τε καὶ ἡλικίαν καὶ ἕψησιν.] Κατὰ γάρ τοι τὴν δευτέραν τάξιν τῶν μαλακτικῶν οὐχ ἥκιστα ἄν τις θείη τὴν ὑπὸ τοῦ Μνασαίου συντεθεῖσαν ἔμπλαστρον, ὁμολογούντων μὲν ἁπάντων ἀλλήλοις τῶν μνημονευσάντων αὐτῆς, ἐξ ἐλαίου τοῦ παλαιοῦ καὶ λιθαργύρου καὶ στέατος ὑείου. διαφερομένων δὲ ἔν τε τῇ ποσότητι τούτων ἑκάστου καὶ τῷ τινὰς μὲν ἐκ προσφάτου στέατος, ἐνίους δὲ ἐκ παλαιοῦ συντιθέναι τοῦ καλουμένου πρὸς ἁπάντων ἤδη συνήθως ἀξουγγίου. ἐπὶ γάρ τοι τούτοις ἡ ἀσθενεστάτη γένοιτ’ ἂν ἐκ λιθαργύρου καὶ στέατος ὑείου καὶ ἐλαίου, μηδετέρου παλαιοῦ. ἡ δ’ ἰσχυροτέρα ἐλαίου μὲν αὐτῶν παλαιοῦ, θατέρου δὲ νέου βληθέντος. ἡ δὲ ταύτης ἰσχυροτέρα παλαιῶν ἀμφοτέρων. κατὰ μὲν οὖν τὸ νέον ἢ παλαιὸν βάλλεσθαι τὸ ἔλαιον καὶ τὸ στέαρ αἱ διαφοραὶ δ΄.

It is therefore safest to write accurately of the difference of the drugs themselves in relation to one another in respect of more and less, 962 just as I have done in the other cases and shall do now. [That different differences in respect of more and less arise in the emollient plaster of Mnaseas according to the differing quantity, age and boiling of the simples.] For indeed in the second rank of the emollients one would not least place the plaster composed by Mnaseas, since all who have mentioned it agree with one another that it is made from old oil, litharge and pig's fat, but differ both in the quantity of each of these and in that some compose it from fresh fat, others from old fat, which is now customarily called by everyone axoungion. For on these grounds the weakest would be that made from litharge, pig's fat and oil, neither of the latter being old; the stronger, when of these the oil is old but the other is put in new; and the one stronger than this, when both are old. Accordingly, with respect to the oil and the fat being put in new or old, the differences will be four:
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὐτῶν (in ἐλαίου μὲν αὐτῶν παλαιοῦ), ἐπὶ γάρ τοι τούτοις, οὐχ ἥκιστα ἄν τις θείη
It is therefore safest to set down accurately the difference of the drugs themselves relative to one another in respect of the more and the less, 962 as I have done in the other cases and shall do now. [That various differences in respect of the more and the less arise in the emollient plaster of Mnasaeus, according to the differing quantity, age and boiling of the simple ingredients.] For in the second order of emollients one might quite justifiably place the plaster composed by Mnasaeus. All who have recorded it agree with one another that it is made of old oil, litharge and pig's fat. They differ, however, both in the quantity of each of these and in that some compose it from fresh fat and others from old, the fat which is now customarily called by everyone axungia. On these premises the weakest would be the one made of litharge, pig's fat and oil, neither of the two being old. The stronger is the one in which the oil is old and the other ingredient, the fat, is put in new. The one stronger than this is the one in which both are old. So, according to whether the oil and the fat are put in new or old, there are four differences.

963 ἔσονται ποτὲ μὲν ἀμφοῖν νέων, ποτὲ δὲ ἀμφοῖν παλαιῶν, ποτὲ δὲ τοῦ μὲν ἐλαίου παλαιοῦ, τοῦ στέατος δὲ προσφάτου ἢ τοὐναντίον, ἐλαίου μὲν προσφάτου, παλαιοῦ δὲ στέατος ἐμβαλλομένου. κατὰ δὲ τὴν πρὸς ἄλληλα συμμετρίαν αὐτῶν τῶν τριῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἕτεραι πλείους. ἤτοι γὰρ ἴσα τῷ σταθμῷ καὶ μέτρῳ μίγνυται τὰ τρία καὶ πολλοί φασι τὴν ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ τοῦ Μνασαίου συντεθεῖσαν ἐκ τριῶν ἴσων συγκεῖσθαι ἢ τὰ δύο μὲν ἴσα, τὸ τρίτον δὲ οὐκ ἴσον ἀλλ’ ἔλαττον ἢ πλέον ἢ τῶν τριῶν ἀνίσων μιγνυμένων. εὔδηλον οὖν ὅτι πολλαὶ γενήσονται διαφοραί. πλέονος μὲν γὰρ τῆς λιθαργύρου βληθείσης μαλακώτερον τῇ δυνάμει γενήσεται τὸ φάρμακον, ἐλάττονος δὲ ἰσχυρότερον. καὶ μαλακτικώτερον μὲν, εἰ τοῦ στέατος ἐμβάλλοιμεν πλέον, διαφορητικώτερον δ’ εἰ τοῦ ἐλαίου. καὶ γὰρ αὖ καὶ ἄλλο τι προσέρχεται πλέονος ἐμβληθέντος ἐλαίου, μεγάλην ἔχοντος δύναμιν εἰς τὸ διαφορητικώτερον ἐργάσασθαι τὸ φάρμακον. ἔστι δὲ τοῦτο, καθάπερ εἴρηταί μοι κατὰ τὸν α΄. λόγον, εἰ ἐπὶ πλέον ἑψήσεις. ἐὰν γοῦν μόνην τὴν λιθάργυρον ἑψήσεις ἐν ἐλαίῳ πλέονι, ξηραντικώτερον ἔσται τὸ φάρμακον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
963 will be: sometimes with both fresh, sometimes with both old, sometimes with the oil old but the fat fresh, or the reverse, with fresh oil but old fat put in. And according to the proportion of these three simple drugs themselves to one another there are several further differences. For either the three are mixed in equal amounts by weight and measure—and many say that the one compounded by Mnaseas himself is composed of three equal parts—or two are equal and the third not equal but less or more, or the three are mixed in unequal amounts. It is quite evident, then, that many differences will arise. For when more litharge is put in, the drug will become milder in its power, and when less, stronger. And it will be more emollient if we put in more of the fat, but more discutient if more of the oil. For indeed something else also comes in when more oil is put in, which has great power to make the drug more discutient; and this is, as I said in the first book, if you boil it for longer. At any rate, if you boil the litharge alone in a greater quantity of oil, the drug will be more drying.

963 the differences will be four: sometimes when both are new, sometimes when both are old, sometimes when the oil is old but the fat fresh, or the reverse, when the oil is fresh but old fat is put in. It is quite clear, then, that many differences will arise. For when more of the litharge is put in, the drug will become softer in its power, and when less, stronger. For when more oil has been put in, something else in turn comes in as well, having great power to make the drug more discutient. This is, as I said in the first book, if you boil it for longer. At any rate, if you boil the litharge alone in a larger quantity of oil, the drug will be more drying.

and 964 you will boil it for longer when you have put in more oil, if indeed it is going to have the consistency of a plaster. For if someone were not to attend to this, but were to boil for an equal time both that which has less oil and that which has more (κατὰ τὸ πλέον), it is obvious that the one with which more oil has been mixed will be more emollient in its power, just as if someone, having mixed raw litharge with oil, should wish to use it as an akopon. Wherefore I bid you always keep in mind what has already often been said by me: that we should not, as some make their pronouncements about drugs without qualification, act in the same way ourselves, saying without qualification that this composition of a drug is better than that one. For each of the two can be well composed for affections differing in kind, and sometimes for the same affection at different occasions. Accordingly, anyone who has first heard all these things now said by me, being not only industrious or retentive in memory but also intelligent by nature, will read with profit what has been written and what will be written by me with distinctions.

And I shall also tell you the distinctions in the case of the drugs under consideration, which come about through the addition of three or four syllables, 965 an addition of just the kind that Andromachus appears to have made in the case of the drug of Mnaseas that is under consideration in the present discussion. This, then, is what I would have expected him to have done for all of them, and, in the case of the quince-yellow plasters themselves, to have begun from the softest one, and, passing from it to the one that is a little stronger, to have made this clear by adding in writing that the second is stronger than this by some small degree, and that the third in turn is stronger than that, and then the fourth than that one.

967 [Τὰ ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου μαλάγματα.] Ἀμυθάωνος μάλαγμα πρὸς τὰς τῶν ὑποχονδρίων διατάσεις λύει πᾶσαν σκληρίαν, ποιεῖ καὶ πρὸς τὰ δυσκίνητα τῶν ἄρθρων. ♃ ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος γο λστ΄. κηροῦ γο λστ΄. τερμινθίνης γο η΄. βδελλίου γο η΄. λιβανωτοῦ οὐγγίας δ΄. σμύρνης γο δ΄. χαλβάνης γο η΄. κυπρίνου κο. δ΄. βρέχεται σμύρνα, λίβανος, βδέλλιον οἴνῳ, τὸ δὲ ἀμμωνιακὸν ὄξει λύεται. πάντα δὲ κόπτεται, τοῦ ὑπέρου χριομένου τῷ κυπρίνῳ μέχρι παντελοῦς διαλύσεως. τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον τῶν παλαιῶν ἐστι καὶ ἐνδόξων, ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ μετὰ τοῦτο γεγραμμένον ὑπὸ τοῦ Ἀσκληπιάδου, κατὰ τόνδε τὸν τρόπον. διάσμυρνον ἀπολοφόνιον, φάρμακον ἐπιτετευγμένον. πρὸς τὰς τῶν ὑποχονδρίων διατάσεις διαλύει πᾶσαν σκληρίαν, ποιεῖ ἡπατικοῖς, σπληνικοῖς, νεφριτικοῖς, ποιεῖ πρὸς πᾶσαν νευρικὴν συμπάθειαν πρὸς τὰς τῶν ἄρθρων ὀδύνας καὶ κατοχὰς ποιεῖ. ♃ κηροῦ γο ιστ΄. τερμινθίνης γο ιστ΄. ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος γο ιστ΄. βδελλίου γο ιστ΄. σμύρνης γο ιστ΄. προπόλεως γο ιστ΄.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπολοφόνιον
967 [The malagmata of Asclepiades.] The malagma of Amythaon for distensions of the hypochondria: it dissolves every hardness, and it works also for joints that are difficult to move. Take: ammoniac incense, 36 ounces; wax, 36 ounces; terebinth, 8 ounces; bdellium, 8 ounces; frankincense, 4 ounces; myrrh, 4 ounces; galbanum, 8 ounces; henna oil, 4 kotylai. The myrrh, frankincense and bdellium are soaked in wine, while the ammoniac is dissolved in vinegar. All are pounded, the pestle being smeared with the henna oil, until complete dissolution. This drug is one of the ancient and celebrated ones, as is also the one written after it by Asclepiades, in the following manner. The diasmyrnon apolophonion (?), a successful drug. For distensions of the hypochondria it dissolves every hardness; it works for hepatic, splenic and nephritic patients; it works for every nervous sympathy; it works for pains and fixations of the joints. Take: wax, 16 ounces; terebinth, 16 ounces; ammoniac incense, 16 ounces; bdellium, 16 ounces; myrrh, 16 ounces; propolis, 16 ounces.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀπολοφόνιον, κατοχὰς, γο, κο.
967 [The emollients (malagmata) recorded by Asclepiades.] The emollient of Amythaon, for distensions of the hypochondria. It dissolves all hardness, and it is also effective for joints that move with difficulty. Take: of ammoniacum incense, 36 oz.; of wax, 36 oz.; of terebinth, 8 oz.; of bdellium, 8 oz.; of frankincense, 4 ounces; of myrrh, 4 oz.; of galbanum, 8 oz.; of oil of cypros, 4 cotylae. The myrrh, frankincense and bdellium are steeped in wine, and the ammoniacum is dissolved in vinegar. Everything is pounded, the pestle being smeared with the oil of cypros, until it is completely dissolved. This drug is one of the old and esteemed ones, as is also the one written after it by Asclepiades, in the following manner. The myrrh preparation (diasmyrnon) of Apollophanes (?), a successful drug. For distensions of the hypochondria; it dissolves all hardness; it is effective for those with liver complaints, those with spleen complaints and those with kidney complaints; it is effective for every sympathetic affection of the sinews; it is effective for pains and fixations of the joints. Take: of wax, 16 oz.; of terebinth, 16 oz.; of ammoniacum incense, 16 oz.; of bdellium, 16 oz.; of myrrh, 16 oz.; of propolis, 16 oz.

968 ἴριδος γο ιστ΄. στύρακος γο ιστ΄. ἰρίνου μύρου ὑποστάθμης ὅσον ἐξαρκεῖ, οἴνου εὐώδους τὸ ἱκανὸν, σκεύαζε καθὰ προείρηται. καὶ τὸ μετὰ τοῦτο δὲ γεγραμμένον ὑπὸ τοῦ Ἀσκληπιάδου φάρμακον ἀξιολογώτερον μὲν ὑπάρχει τῶν ὑπογεγραμμένων, ἧττον δὲ ἐκείνων εἰς τὴν τῶν ἐσκληρυσμένων λύσιν. ἄλλο Διοσκορίδου φάρμακον ἐπιτετευγμένον πρὸς τὰ δυσκίνητα κῶλα καὶ πρὸς ἀγκύλας διαλύει πᾶσαν σκληρίαν, ποιεῖ καὶ πρὸς τὰς τῶν ἐντὸς διαθέσεις καὶ πρὸς τὰς ἐν βάθει ἀποστάσεις. ♃ κηροῦ μνᾶν α΄. ῥητίνης φρυκτῆς μνᾶς ἥμισυ, χαλβάνης μνᾶς ἥμισυ, ἴριδος Ἰλλυρικῆς μνᾶς ἥμισυ, ἰρίνου ὑποστάθμης τὸ ἱκανὸν, οἴνου ὅσον ἐξαρκεῖ, κοπτόν ἐστι τὸ φάρμακον, σκεύαζε καθὰ προείρηται. τὸ μετὰ τὸ προειρημένον φάρμακον ἐφεξῆς γεγραμμένον ὑπὸ Ἀσκληπιάδου ταὐτὸν ὑπάρχει τῷ ὑπὸ τοῦ Ἥρα γεγραμμένῳ κατὰ τὸ τέλος τῆς φαρμακίτιδος αὐτοῦ βίβλου, βραχεῖαν δέ τινα διαφορὰν ἐν τῇ συμμετρίᾳ τῶν τεττάρων ἔχει φαρμάκων ἁπλῶν, ὡς ἔσται φανερὸν, ὅταν ἀμφότερα γραφῇ.

968 iris 16 ounces, storax 16 ounces, of the sediment of iris perfume as much as suffices, of fragrant wine a sufficient quantity; prepare as stated above. And the drug written by Asclepiades after this one is indeed more noteworthy than those written below, but less effective than they are for the dissolution of hardened parts. Another, of Dioscorides, a well-tested drug for limbs that move with difficulty and for ankyloses; it dissolves every hardness, and it is effective also for the conditions of the inner parts and for abscesses in depth. Take wax 1 mina, roasted (phrykte) resin half a mina, galbanum half a mina, Illyrian iris half a mina, of the sediment of iris oil a sufficient quantity, of wine as much as suffices; the drug is a pounded one; prepare as stated above. But first I shall mention the one written by Asclepiades, 969 which runs word for word as follows. The drug of Lucius the teacher, inscribed 'for ankylai'. It works for abscesses deep down; it works also for contractions of the sinews and for the fixations of the joints, which we call ankylai. It works for those with opisthotonos and for every sympathetic affection of the nerves. It is also a good acopon, when dissolved in iris-oil or cyprus-oil or laurel-oil. It works for chronic and hard-to-heal indurations. Take of bdellos 16 drachms, of calf suet 16 drachms, of ammoniac incense 16 drachms, of Illyrian iris 16 drachms, of opopanax 8 drachms, of galbanum 8 drachms, of cachrys 8 drachms, of storax 8 drachms, of frankincense 8 drachms, of pepper 160 grains, of the dregs of iris-unguent as much as suffices, of wax and of terebinth half a litra each, of wine a sufficient amount; the drug is a pounded one; prepare as has been stated before. Where, then, Heras differed from Asclepiades in the proportion of the simples, I shall now show, as I promised. First of all, Asclepiades wrote as a drug 16 holkai of bdellos; but Heras not bdellos, but 80 holkai of bdellium.

But first I shall record the one written by Asclepiades, 969 which reads word for word as follows. 'Entitled "Of Lucius the preceptor, for ankylai".' It acts on deep-seated abscessions; it also acts on contractions of the sinews and on the fixations of the joints which we call ankylai. It acts for the opisthotonic and against every sympathetic affection of the sinews. It is also a good akopon, dissolved in iris oil or cyprinum or laurel oil. The drug is a pounded one; prepare it as stated before. Where, then, Heras disagreed with Asclepiades in the proportion of the simples, I shall now show, as I promised. As the very first ingredient Asclepiades wrote bdellus, 16 holkai; but Heras wrote not bdellus but bdellium, 80 holkai.

Greek (Kühn)
πενταπλάσιαι 970 δέ εἰσιν αὗται τῶν ιστ΄. δηλονότι. ἐφεξῆς δὲ στέατος μοσχείου ὁλκὰς ιστ΄. γράψαντος τοῦ Ἀσκληπιάδου, πενταπλασίας ὁ Ἥρας ἔγραψεν, ὡς καὶ τοῦ βδελλίου. πενταπλάσιαι γὰρ δηλονότι αἱ π΄. δραχμαὶ τῶν ι΄. καὶ στ΄. εἰσί. τρίτον ἐπὶ τοῖς γεγραμμένοις ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης ἔγραψε φάρμακον, ἀμμωνιακὸν θυμίαμα καὶ τούτου τὰς δραχ. ιστ΄. ἀξιῶν βάλλεσθαι. κατὰ τὴν ἀναλογίαν οὖν τῶν ἔμπροσθεν ἐχρῆν καὶ τὸν Ἥραν π΄. γεγραφέναι δραχ.. ἔγραψε δ’ οὐ τοσαύτας, ἀλλὰ ρ΄. ὥστε πλέονας ἐνέβαλεν κ΄. ἄλλο δ’ ἐπὶ τοῖς προγεγραμμένοις φαρμάκοις ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης, οὕτως εἰπὼν, ἴρεως Ἰλλυρικῆς δραχ. ιστ΄. ἐφύλαξε δὲ τούτου τὴν ἀναλογίαν ὁ Ἥρας π΄. γράψας. ἐπὶ τοῖς προγεγραμμένοις φαρμάκοις ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης ὀποπάνακος δραχ. η΄. ἐφύλαξε δὲ καὶ τὴν πρὸς τοῦτο ἀναλογίαν ὁ Ἥρας μ΄. γράψας ἕκτου φαρμάκου, χαλβάνης ἐμνημόνευσεν ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης δραχ. η΄. ἐμβαλών. ἐφύλαξε καὶ τὴν πρὸς αὐτὴν ἀναλογίαν ὁ Ἥρας, πενταπλασίαν ἀξιῶν ἐμβάλλεσθαι. πενταπλάσιαι γάρ εἰσιν αἱ μ΄. τῶν η΄. ἕβδομον φάρμακον ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης ἔγραψεν οὕτως.

And these are fivefold 970, obviously, of the sixteen. Next, whereas Asclepiades wrote sixteen drachms of calf's fat, Heras wrote five times as many, as also in the case of the bdellium; for the eighty drachms are, obviously, fivefold of the sixteen. As a third drug after those already written Asclepiades wrote ammoniac incense, deeming that sixteen drachms of this too be put in. According to the proportion of the preceding ones, therefore, Heras too ought to have written eighty drachms; but he wrote not so many, but a hundred, so that he put in twenty more. Another drug after the drugs written before Asclepiades wrote, speaking thus: of Illyrian iris, sixteen drachms; and Heras preserved the proportion of this, writing eighty. After the drugs written before, Asclepiades wrote opopanax, eight drachms; and Heras preserved the proportion with respect to this too, writing forty. Of a sixth drug, galbanum, Asclepiades made mention, putting in eight drachms; Heras preserved the proportion with respect to it as well, deeming that five times as much be put in; for forty is fivefold of eight. of cachrys 971 8 drachmas. So Heras too, if he were preserving the proportion, ought to have put in 40; but as it is, not so, but twice as many, for he himself has written 16. As the eighth, Asclepiades mentions storax, putting in 8 drachmas; Heras preserved the proportion of this, putting in fivefold. As the ninth drug Asclepiades wrote frankincense, putting in 8 drachmas of it, but Heras requires not fivefold but double. As the tenth Asclepiades mentioned pepper, putting in 160 grains, and in agreement with him again Heras put in 500. In some of the copies, I know not how, 20 are written, just as in others 108. Greek (Kühn)
εὔδηλον οὖν ὅτι τοῦ μὲν κάγχρυος ὁ Ἥρας ἐμβάλλει 972 διπλάσιον τῆς ἀναλογίας, ὥσπερ καὶ τοῦ λιβανωτοῦ. τοῦ δὲ ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος ἐπλεόνασεν ἐν τῷ τετάρτῳ μέρει, καθάπερ γε καὶ τῆς τερμινθίνης ῥητίνης μετὰ τοῦ κηροῦ γ΄. καὶ γὰρ ἐπὶ τούτων ἐπλεόνασε τῷ τρίτῳ μέρει καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο παραπλησίαν ἐποίησε τὴν ὅλην σύνθεσιν τοῦ φαρμάκου τῇ τοῦ Ἀσκληπιάδου. πλεονάσας γὰρ ἐν τῇ τοῦ κάγχρυος συμμετρίᾳ μηδὲν ἔχοντος μαλακὸν, ἀλλὰ μόνον διαφορητικὸν, συνηύξησεν αὐτῷ καὶ τὰ μαλακτικὰ, τόν τε λιβανωτὸν καὶ τὸν κηρὸν καὶ τὴν τερμινθίνην. τοῦτο μὲν οὖν τὸ φάρμακον Λευκίου μὲν ὀνομάζει τοῦ καθηγητοῦ Ἀσκληπιάδης, ὅπῃ δὲ διαφέρεται τῷ Ἥρᾳ διῆλθον. ἐσχάτης δὲ τάξεώς ἐστι τῶν μαλακτικῶν, οὐ γὰρ ἂν εὕροις αὐτοῦ μαλακτικώτερον τῶν σκιῤῥουμένων. ἐστόχασται δὲ ὁ συνθεὶς αὐτὸ καὶ τοῦ διαφορεῖν τὸ περιεχόμενον ἐν τοῖς σκιῤῥουμένοις. τὰ δὲ ἄλλα φάρμακα τὰ πρὸ αὐτοῦ βραχὺ πλεονεκτεῖ τῶν προγεγραμμένων. ἐν αὐτοῖς δὲ περιέχεται καὶ τὰ τμητικὰ τῶν πεπαχυμμένων καὶ γλίσχρων χυμῶν, ἃ πολλάκις γίγνεται χρήσιμα τοῖς σκιῤῥουμένοις ὄγκοις, ὅταν ὑπὸ γλίσχρων χυμῶν ἡ σκίῤῥωσις αὐτῶν κατασκευάζηται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: γ΄ (after μετὰ τοῦ κηροῦ, taken as 'by a third'), τὰ πρὸ αὐτοῦ
It is quite evident, then, that of the cachrys Heras puts in 972 double the proportion, just as also of the frankincense. But of the ammoniac incense he exceeded by a fourth part, just as indeed also of the terebinth resin together with the wax by a third; for in the case of these too he exceeded by a third part, and in this respect he made the whole composition of the drug similar to that of Asclepiades. For having exceeded in the proportion of the cachrys, which has nothing soft in it but is only dispersive, he increased along with it the emollients as well, the frankincense and the wax and the terebinth. This drug, then, Asclepiades names as that of Lucius the teacher; and in what respects it differs from Heras I have gone through. It is of the highest rank among the emollients, for you would not find anything more emollient than it for parts becoming scirrhous. And the one who composed it aimed also at dispersing what is contained in the parts becoming scirrhous. The other drugs before it have a slight advantage over those written earlier. And among them are included also the incisive agents for thickened and viscous humours, which often become useful for tumours that are becoming scirrhous, whenever their scirrhosis is brought about by viscous humours.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γ΄, ἐσχάτης δὲ τάξεώς, βραχὺ πλεονεκτεῖ τῶν προγεγραμμένων
It is therefore quite clear that Heras puts in 972 double the proportionate amount of cachrys, as also of frankincense. Of ammoniac incense he has exceeded by a fourth part, as indeed also of terebinth resin together with the wax (3). For in these too he exceeded by a third part, and in this way he made the whole composition of the drug closely similar to that of Asclepiades. For, having exceeded in the measure of cachrys, which has nothing softening but is only discutient, he increased along with it the emollient ingredients as well: the frankincense, the wax and the terebinth. Asclepiades names this drug as that of Lucius his teacher, and I have gone through the points in which he differs from Heras. It is of the highest rank among the emollients, for you would not find one more emollient than it for parts becoming scirrhous. Whoever composed it also aimed at dispersing what is contained in the parts becoming scirrhous. The other drugs before it surpass those written earlier by a little. Among them are also included the agents that cut thickened and viscid humours, which are often useful for scirrhous swellings, when their scirrhous induration is produced by viscid humours.

Opopanax, then, attenuates 973 moderately. They call by this name the root of the panax, whose stalk they tap for juice to make opopanax; for it is of a cleansing, not of an incisive power. But vinegar together with goat's dung cuts and cleanses and disperses powerfully not only thickened and viscous humours but also the solid bodies themselves; these are added in the drug written next after the one before us, which he says is inscribed as Alcimion's. He writes, then, about the former in the following manner. Another malagma of Marcus Telentius Asclepiades, exceedingly noble against the aforesaid conditions. It works especially against inflations of the womb; the drug is inscribed 'Asclepiadeion' because it is referred back to Asclepiades the Bithynian. Take myrrh, 3 drachms, or otherwise 3 ounces; opopanax, half a litra; wax, one litra and a half; galbanum, 3 ounces; manna, half a litra; ammoniac incense, half a litra; roasted resin, one litra and a half; vinegar, one kotyle. It is to be used also for every nervous sympathy. Another, inscribed as Alcimion's. Take wax, one litra

Moderately, 973 then, does opopanax thin. They call by this name the root of the panax, from whose stalk they make opopanax by drawing off its juice; for it is of a detergent, not an incisive, faculty. Vinegar together with goat's dung cuts, cleanses and disperses powerfully not only thickened and viscid humours but also the solid bodies themselves; these are added to the drug written next after the one under discussion, which he says is entitled 'of Alcimion'. About the former, then, he writes in this manner: Another emollient of Marcus Telentius Asclepiades, very excellent for the aforementioned conditions. It acts especially against inflations of the womb; the drug is entitled 'Asclepiadeion' because it is ascribed to Asclepiades the Bithynian. Take: myrrh, 3 drachms (otherwise, 3 ounces); opopanax, a half-litra; wax, 1½ litrae; galbanum, 3 ounces; manna, a half-litra; ammoniac incense, a half-litra; roasted resin, 1½ litrae; vinegar, 1 kotyle. It is to be used also for every sympathetic affection of the sinews. Another, entitled 'of Alcimion'. Take: wax, 1 litra

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μετὰ τὴν χαλβάνην, συστραφῇ
974 and a half, of Colophonian resin one pound and a half, of ammoniac incense one pound and a half, of galbanum three ounces, of myrrh three ounces, of frankincense three ounces, of opopanax one pound and a half, of propolis three ounces, of vinegar two xestai, of goat's dung three ounces. Grind the ammoniac, adding one xestes of vinegar; then add to these the myrrh, the frankincense, the opopanax, and triturate again. Pound the propolis together with the galbanum. Boil the wax and the resin, adding two kotylai of oil, and when it has thickened together, add the propolis and the galbanum, stirring continuously; and when it has dissolved, take it from the fire and let it cool, and having scraped it off, add it to the triturated ingredients and, having mixed them, take it up and use it. After the malagma written above there is written in the book of Asclepiades another, having a little emollient power, but for the most part heating. For it contains not only the juice of gentian, but also the saps, both that of peucedanum and that of sagapenum, drugs which heat vehemently.

974 and a half; Colophonian resin, 1½ pounds; ammoniacum incense, 1½ pounds; galbanum, 3 ounces; myrrh, 3 ounces; frankincense, 3 ounces; opopanax, 1½ pounds; propolis, 3 ounces; vinegar, 2 sextarii; goat's dung, 3 ounces. Pound the ammoniacum, adding 1 sextarius of vinegar; then add to these the myrrh, the frankincense and the opopanax, and grind smooth again. Pound the propolis together with the galbanum. Boil the wax and the resin, adding 2 cotylae of oil; and when it has drawn together, add the propolis and galbanum, stirring continuously. And when it has melted, take it off the fire and let it cool, and having scraped it off, add it to the ingredients that have been ground smooth, and having mixed, take it up and use. After the emollient plaster written above, there is written in the book of Asclepiades another, which has little emollient power but more heating power. It contains not only the juice of gentian but also the saps, both that of peucedanum and that of sagapenum, drugs that heat strongly.

There is mixed into it also aphronitron and flower of Asian stone, drugs of attenuating 975 and cutting power. And that unburnt sulphur heats, everyone knows, just as indeed also pitch-wine (pissites). I shall append this drug too in Asclepiades' own wording. A malagma inscribed 'Pharmianon', for the aforesaid conditions. It is especially effective for pleuritics, for it relieves the pains at once when applied during the paroxysms themselves. After the application of the drug, when redness has arisen around the places, one must lift off the splenion and, after allowing an interval, apply it again; it relieves also the conditions that have become chronic. Take wax, 5 litrai, or 1 litra; ammoniac incense, 3 litrai; roasted (phrykte) resin, 3 unciae, or 1 litra; terebinth, 1 litra; frankincense, 1½ litrai; myrrh, 6 unciae; aloe, 6 unciae; oesypum, 3 unciae; juice of gentian, 3 unciae; juice of peucedanum, 3 unciae, or opopanax, 6 unciae; sagapenum, 4 unciae; aphronitron, 6 unciae; flower of Asian stone, 6 unciae; propolis, 3 unciae; unburnt sulphur, 4 unciae; pitch-wine, 5 xestai. of ammoniac incense 978 8 drachmas, of terebinth 50 drachmas, of fresh wax 100 drachmas, of henna oil half a kotyle, of vinegar a sufficient quantity. Some give of ammoniac incense 100 drachmas. For all internal conditions. The one made with seeds. ♃ Of ammoniac incense 50 drachmas, of wax 50 drachmas, of terebinth 50 drachmas, of fenugreek 25 drachmas, of prepared bull's suet 25 drachmas, of Illyrian iris 25 drachmas, of nitron 25 drachmas, of honey 6 kotylai, of vinegar 1 kotyle, of panax 25 drachmas, of henna oil a little. But as Philonides gives it: ♃ Of fenugreek seed, of anise seed, of storax, of unwashed bdellium, of manna, of frankincense, of cardamom, of fresh cyperus, of panax root, of Illyrian iris, of aphronitron, of each 25 drachmas apiece; of ammoniac incense, of terebinth, 50 drachmas each; of bull's suet 60 drachmas, of melilot 60 drachmas, of wax 2 drachmas, otherwise 100; of honey half a kotyle, of wine 1 kotyle. Another, as Harpocras gives it: ♃ Of fenugreek, of nitron, of panax, of iris, 8 drachmas each; of terebinth, of ammoniac incense, of wax, 100 drachmas each; of bull's suet 100 drachmas, otherwise 50; of honey 1 litra, of vinegar 2 xestai.

The plaster with alum, 980 another version. Take: wax, 100 dr.; pitch, 50 dr.; liquid alum, 25 dr. (but I [use] 50 dr.); mistletoe-glue (ixos), 50 dr. A good one for sciatic patients. Take: seed of wild rue, silphium, bay-berries, aphronitron, southernwood, colocynth, cardamom, ammi, 4 dr. each; green rue, pitch, terebinth resin, ammoniac incense, wax, fat, 8 dr. each; galbanum, 6 dr.; opopanax, 4 dr.; unburnt sulphur, 4 dr. For splenetics. Take: wax, 1 mina; resin, 1 mina; ammoniac incense, half a mina; liquid alum, 50 dr.; strigil-grease (gloios), 1 cotyle; caper root, 50 dr.; calamint root, 50 dr.; myrobalan dregs (magma), 50 dr. Use vinegar for the dry ingredients, oil for the meltable ones. A malagma of many uses. Take: mustard, 4 dr.; manna, 2 dr.; rue, 4 dr.; aphronitron, 4 dr.; caper root, 6 dr.; ammoniac incense, 4 dr.; myrobalan, 25 dr.; the fine centaury, 4 dr. or 7; flower of Asian stone, 4 dr.; fenugreek, 4 dr.; cardamom, 4 dr.; wax, 75 dr.; resin, 100 dr.; honey, 6 cotylai; sediment of iris-oil, 1 cotyle.

of rose-oil 986 4 cotylae, of fenugreek unguent 1 cotyle, of good olive oil 1 cotyle. Melt these over a gentle fire, stirring lightly. Greek (Kühn)
♃ 988 ἀῤῥενικοῦ γο γ΄. στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς ἴσον, ἀσβέστου τὸ ἴσον, ῥητίνης φρυκτῆς λίτρας γ΄. κηροῦ ἴσον, ὄξους εἰς τὰ ξηρὰ, ἐλαίου εἰς τὰ τηκτὰ γο η΄. χρῶ καὶ ἐπὶ σπληνικῶν. [Δαμοκράτους μάλαγμα.] Ἐπεὶ καὶ πρὸς μνήμην ἐστὶ χρήσιμα τὰ διὰ μέτρων γεγραμμένα καὶ τὰς συμμετρίας τῶν φαρμάκων ἀκριβῶς φυλάττει, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τὰ Δαμοκράτους εἴωθα προσγράφειν, ἐν οἷς ἐστι καὶ τοῦτο προσγεγραμμένον. Χρεία δὲ πάντως καὶ μαλάγματος καλοῦ Τοῖς σφόδρα φιλιάτροισιν ἔσται πολλάκις Πρὸς σπάσματα μυῶν μεγάλα καὶ πρὸς ῥήγματα Ἄρθρων. ἔτι δὲ καὶ λυγίσματα, Νεύρων παχυσμοὺς καὶ μυῶν τὰς σκληρίας Μετὰ φλεγμονῆς, πρὸς μέντοι καὶ τὰ δήγματα, Πρὸς ταῦτα τοῦτό ἐστι δραστικώτατον. Rendering A (fable-5-1)
991 Then after this he writes another one too, made with salts, and after both of these again another, one that professes to disperse scrofulous swellings (choirades) and dropsical conditions of the liver and of the spleen. But it seemed better to me to write these drugs among the discutient plasters. [On relaxing drugs.] Certain drugs are customarily called "relaxing" (chalastic) by almost all the more recent physicians, drugs in the case of which, as the name itself shows, they relax the parts that are tensed. For "to be relaxed" is said as the contrary of "to be tensed." At any rate it has been written somewhere by Hippocrates too as follows: "of hard skin, softening; of tensed skin, relaxation." If, then, one were to use the names in their proper sense, one will say that certain drugs are emollients of hardened bodies, namely those that dissolve their hardness, just as the relaxing ones are curative of tension.

991 Then after this he also writes another drug made with salts, and after both of them yet another, which professes to disperse scrofulous swellings (choirades) and dropsical conditions of the liver and spleen. To me, however, it seemed better to write these drugs up among the discutient plasters. [On relaxing drugs.] Certain drugs are called 'relaxing' (chalastika) in the customary usage of almost all the more recent physicians: drugs by which, as the name itself also shows, the parts that have been stretched are relaxed. For 'being relaxed' is said as the opposite of 'being drawn tight'. At any rate, it has been written somewhere by Hippocrates as follows: 'of hard skin, softening; of tightened skin, relaxation.' If, then, one were to use the words in their proper sense, one will say that certain drugs are emollient of bodies that have become hardened, namely those that dissolve their hardness, just as certain drugs are relaxing, namely those curative of tension.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Now it happens in inflammations that the skin is stretched, sometimes receiving this symptom from the underlying swelling, and sometimes also itself being inflamed along with it; yet the essence of the inflammation is not 992 the hardness of the inflamed part but, entirely to the contrary, as we show in our discussions concerning it, moistness. In parts that are becoming scirrhous, however, it is not moistness that prevails in the affected part, but hardness. Of both affections the genesis and the cure are in a certain way of the same kind: for they are generated from an influx, and they are cured when these [humours] have been evacuated. Yet the manner of the evacuation is not the same in both; rather, as has been shown in the treatise on the Therapeutic Method, in which the method of cure of all the other affections too has been stated, in the case of inflamed parts the repellent quality of the drugs is beneficial during the first periods, and the discutient quality throughout; whereas in scirrhi the discutient quality alone is beneficial, without the repellent. Now those parts that have become completely scirrhous are completely incurable; and one must diagnose them by the fact that no sensation at all remains in the affected parts.

It happens that in inflammations the skin is stretched, sometimes acquiring this symptom from the underlying swelling, and sometimes also being itself inflamed along with it; yet the essence of inflammation 992 is not the hardness of the inflamed part but, quite the contrary, as we show in our discussions of it, moistness. ἐφ’ ὧν δ’ ἐστὶ μὲν αἴσθησις, ἀλλ’ ἀμυδρὰ, θεραπεύεται ταῦτα διὰ φαρμάκων διαφορούντων μὲν δηλονότι τὸ παχυνθὲν ὑγρὸν, οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρῶς γε καὶ ἀθρόως 993 τοῦτο ποιούντων, ὡς τὰ διαφορητικὰ ποιοῦσι φάρμακα. καλοῦσι δὲ ἐπισπαστικά τε καὶ ἐξιπωτικὰ τὰ τοιαῦτα. θαυμάζειν δ’ οὐ χρὴ τὴν μετάπτωσιν εἰς σκληρότητα γιγνομένην ἐκ τῶν φλεγμαινόντων, ὅταν ἅμα τό τε ἐπιῤῥυὲν ὑγρὸν ᾖ παχύτερόν τε καὶ γλίσχρον, ἥ τε ἴασις διὰ φαρμάκων γένηται ξηραινόντων σφοδρῶς. ὡς γὰρ κᾀπὶ τῶν ἐκτὸς αὐτῷ μὲν ὁμιλοῦντα τῷ πυρὶ πολλὰ τῶν σωμάτων φρύγεται, δι’ ὕδατος δὲ ἀναμαλάττεται, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον ἐπὶ τῶν φλεγμαινόντων ἡ κατὰ τὸν πεπονθότα τόπον ὑγρότης ὑπὸ μὲν τῶν θερμαινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων ἰσχυρότερον φαρμάκων ὑπολειπόμενον ἴσχει τι γλίσχρον καὶ παχὺ, διαφορηθέντος τοῦ λεπτοῦ. καὶ τούτῳ τῷ τρόπῳ κατά τε τὰς ἀρθρίτιδας οἱ πῶροι γίγνονται καὶ κατὰ τὰς νεφρίτιδας οἱ λίθοι. ἐὰν δέ ποτε τὸ φλεγμονὴν ἐργασόμενον ῥεῦμα λεπτὸν ᾖ κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν, ἥ τε ἴασις αὐτοῦ διὰ τῶν ξηραινόντων καὶ θερμαινόντων γένηται φαρμάκων, οὐδὲν ὑπολείπεται σκιῤῥῶδες. οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ καὶ τὰ σφοδρῶς ψύχοντά τε καὶ στύφοντα τοὺς σκίῤῥους εἴωθεν ἐργάσασθαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Those, however, in which there is sensation, but faint, are treated by means of drugs which do indeed disperse the thickened humour, yet do not do this strongly and all at once, 993 as the discutient drugs do. Such drugs they call epispastic (drawing) and expressive (squeezing-out). One should not wonder at the change into hardness that comes about from inflamed parts, when at the same time the humour that has flowed in is both thicker and viscous, and the cure is effected through drugs that dry violently. For just as, in the case of external things, many bodies when they come into contact with fire itself are parched, but by water are softened again, in the same way, in inflamed parts, the moisture in the affected place, under the action of drugs that heat and dry more strongly, retains something viscous and thick left behind, once the thin part has been dispersed. And it is in this way that in arthritic affections the tophi (poroi) come about, and in nephritic affections the stones. But if ever the flux that is going to produce an inflammation is thin in consistency, and its cure is effected through drying and heating drugs, nothing scirrhous is left behind. And not least, drugs that cool and astringe violently are also accustomed to produce scirrhi.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξιπωτικὰ, πῶροι
But those in which sensation is present, though faint, are treated by means of drugs that do, clearly, disperse the thickened fluid, yet do not do this strongly and all at once 993, as the discutient drugs do. Such drugs are called drawing (epispastic) and squeezing-out (exipōtic). One should not be surprised at the change into hardness that takes place from inflamed parts when, at the same time, the fluid that has flowed in is rather thick and viscous and the cure is effected by drugs that dry vehemently. For just as, in external things too, many bodies are parched when in contact with fire itself, but are softened again by means of water, in the same way, in inflamed parts, the fluid in the affected place, under drugs that heat and dry rather strongly, retains something viscous and thick left behind, the thin part having been dispersed. And it is in this way that the chalk-stones (pōroi) form in arthritic affections and the stones in nephritic affections. But if ever the flux that is going to produce an inflammation is thin in consistency, and its cure is effected by drying and heating drugs, nothing scirrhous is left behind. Not least, drugs that cool and astringe vehemently are also wont to produce scirrhi.

καὶ πολλοὺς ἐξ ἐρυσιπελάτων ἀμέτρως παθόντων ταῦτα σκιῤῥώδεις 994 ὄγκους ἔχοντας θεώμεθα διὰ τὴν ἀμαθίαν τῶν θεραπευτάντων αὐτοὺς ἰατρῶν. πρώτη μὲν οὖν διαφορὰ τῶν τε τὰς φλεγμονὰς θεραπευσάντων φαρμάκων ἐστὶ καὶ τῶν τοὺς σκίῤῥους ἡ εἰρημένη. ἑτέρα δὲ ἡ διὰ τὴν συνεζευγμένην ὀδύνην τοῖς φλεγμαίνουσιν οὐκ ἀνεχομένην ἰσχυρὸν φάρμακον προσφέρεσθαι τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ. σὺν ὀδύνῃ γὰρ ἐνεργεῖ ταῦτα, φθανόντων ὀδυνᾶσθαι τῶν φλεγμαινόντων διὰ τὸ πάθος καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μαλακωτάτων φαρμάκων δεομένων. τὰ σφοδρότερα δὲ πρὸς τῷ μὴ λύειν ἢ παρηγορεῖν τὰς ὀδύνας, ἔτι καὶ παροξύνοντα, μείζονας ἐργάζεται τὰς φλεγμονάς. πρώτη μὲν οὖν χρῆσις ἐπὶ τῶν ὀδυνωμένων φλεγμονῶν ἐστιν ἡ διὰ τῶν ὑγρῶν ἱκανῶς φαρμάκων, ἅμα τῷ μετρίως θερμαίνειν. ἐφεξῆς δὲ ἡ διὰ τῶν ἠρέμα παχυτέρων τε καὶ ξηραντικωτέρων. ἡ τρίτη δὲ διὰ τῶν χαλαστικῶν ὀνομαζομένων, ἅ τινες ἐν τοῖς μαλάγμασι γράφουσιν. ἔστι δὲ αὐτῶν μαλακτικώτατα μὲν ὅσα κηρωτοειδῆ ταῖς συστάσεσιν, ὅταν διὰ βουτύρου καὶ κηροῦ καὶ στεάτων, ἐλαίου τε χαλαστικοῦ συντίθεται.

And we see many people who, from erysipelas that have suffered these things immoderately, have scirrhous 994 tumours, through the ignorance of the physicians who treated them. The first difference, then, between the drugs that treat inflammations and those that treat scirrhi is the one stated. A second is that which arises because of the pain conjoined with inflamed parts, which does not allow a strong drug to be applied to the affected part. For these drugs act with pain, while the inflamed parts are already in pain because of the affection and for this reason require the softest drugs. The more vehement ones, besides not resolving or soothing the pains, even exacerbate them and make the inflammations greater. The first use, then, in the case of painful inflammations is that by means of drugs that are sufficiently moist, together with heating moderately; next, that by means of those that are slightly thicker and more drying; and the third, by means of those called relaxing (chalastic), which some write down among the malagmata. Of these the most emollient are those that are cerate-like in their consistency, when they are compounded from butter and wax and fats and a relaxing oil.

And we see many who, from erysipelases that were immoderately subjected to these [i.e. strongly cooling and astringent applications], have scirrhous 994 swellings, through the ignorance of the physicians who treated them. The first difference, then, between the drugs that treat inflammations and those that treat scirrhi is the one that has been stated. Of these the most emollient are those that are cerate-like in consistency, when they are compounded from butter, wax and fats, and relaxing oil.

And there is mixed with these a little oesypum and resin and 995 pitch, as also frankincense and galbanum and some of the Illyrian iris. And the drug called 'dia chylōn' (through juices), a curative drug for inflammations that are no longer strongly painful, was discovered by Menecrates, as he himself says and as certain others testify on his behalf. The book in which the drug is written up is entitled 'Autokrator hologrammatos' (The Emperor, in Whole Letters): 'Autokrator' because it has been dedicated to him, and 'hologrammatos' because, without numeral signs, two and three and four and five and each of the other numbers, whether of resin or of pitch or of some other drug, are written out in whole syllables. Menecrates did this because it often happens that errors are made in the written texts not only unintentionally, but also deliberately on the part of some out of envy, who make the letter omicron a theta by drawing another, transverse, line through the middle of the round stroke, and the letter iota, likewise, by adding another transverse line, just as also, when one stroke is scraped away from the letter gamma (γράμμα in the text) and from rho, it happens that the shape of iota is left behind.

Menecrates did this because it often happens that errors are made in the writings not only by people acting unintentionally but also by some acting deliberately out of envy: they make the letter ο into θ by drawing another, transverse line through the middle of the round line, and alter the letter iota by adding another, likewise transverse, line; just as, when one line is scraped off from the letter [gamma?] and from rho, the result is that the character of iota is left.

In the same way, in the case of many letters, by addition in some, by combination in others, by subtraction in others, 996 it comes about that the characters signifying them undergo alteration. But Menecrates accomplished nothing more for the time that followed, since those who copied out his book did not write the names of the numbers in full letters, but by means of the characters signifying them. Reasonably, then, the books of Damocrates on drugs, written in metre, have won approval, and if all had been written in this manner, it would have been best. But since not a few of the approved drugs have been left out, for this reason we also need the other pharmaceutical books, in which some have written compositions of varied drugs. But the drug through juices discovered by Menecrates has been written by Damocrates in the following trimeter verses, together with certain others as well, two of them, similar to it, having been written before it.
