[The remedy through juices, of Damocrates, by Menecrates.] Having said, then, before this how wounds that remain without inflammation will be glued together, we shall next say how those that are greatly inflamed 997 it will be possible to treat well with drugs. When, a muscle having been wounded, or some nerves, or even a membrane near a bone having been pricked, after the fourth day there comes an addition of frequent pains and inflammations and fevers with symptoms — much sleeplessness, derangement of mind, violent thirst, dryness of the tongue — inclining day by day towards the worse; a lees-like, thin ichor, red wounds, and all the bodies near them having turgors, distensions, inflammations, oedemata: then do not plaster them with variegated cataplasms. For it is the custom of the young to do this, so that, having escaped the peaks of the inflammations, they may not furnish the slanderers with a cause of accusation. But he who has confidence in his own practice must also go down together with the sick into their fears, not waiting upon fortune and nature.

[The drug made from juices, of Damocrates, (devised) by Menecrates.] Having, then, said earlier how wounds that remain without inflammation will be conglutinated, we shall next say how it will be possible to treat well with drugs those that are severely inflamed. 997 When, after a muscle or some nerves have been wounded, or a membrane near a bone has been pricked, there come after the fourth day frequent pains and inflammations, and fevers with symptoms; when there is an increase of much sleeplessness, delirium, violent thirst and dryness of the tongue, inclining day by day towards the worse; when the ichor is lees-like and thin, the wounds red, and all the parts near them have turgescences, distensions, inflammations and oedemas: then do not apply varied poultices. For it is the custom of the young to do this, so that, having escaped the peaks of the inflammations, they may not give the slanderers cause for accusation. But one who has confidence in his own practice must also go down with the patients into their fears, not waiting upon fortune and nature.

998 Prepare, then, for the affections mentioned above, the drug that is suited also to those with podagra, to be applied to the sick at every season; for in the intervals it provides ready ease of movement, and the not being readily troubled quickly by tophi (πῶροι) and by frequent attacks; at the onsets it does not allow them to take on magnitude, and at the heights it brings about the declines quickly, provided one does not readily shift over to other remedies. Prepare the drug in this manner: having put in two pounds of litharge, very finely ground, of the chrysitis kind, four of very old axungia (lard), and one pound and a third of a pound of oil, many years old and very thick, and not foul-smelling, not black, not rough; for thus the drug will be effective.

998 Prepare, then, for the affections mentioned before, a drug that is also fitting for the gouty, one laid upon the patients at every stage: for in the intervals it affords a ready ease of movement, and the security of not being quickly or easily troubled by chalk-stones (πῶροι) and frequent attacks; in the beginnings it does not allow the affections to take on magnitude, and at the peaks it quickly brings about the declines, provided one does not lightly shift to other remedies. Having carefully melted the axunge in a dish, take off from it the membranes and the salts, 999 straining it through a strainer into another, larger pot; and, having added oil, bring it to the boil again—yet not all the oil, but two parts of it; leaving the third aside, keep it lying in readiness, so that, should you be pressed to add it, it may be at hand for you. And there will certainly be need of it in winter, at any rate, when the oil that is put into the drug has become thick through the long time. And when the drug, having been boiled for a moderate time, has taken on the thickness of a plaster and has likewise taken on the foul smell of a drug, quickly lift the pot from the fire, making it slightly harder in summer and soft in winter, yet nonetheless such that it does not stain; and stir it likewise until it has cooled down moderately, 1000 then again with firmer hands work (πρόσπασσε) the whole of it, once it has been softened, and so transfer it out of the pot. And when there is need, knead it again vigorously, and having carefully spread it upon soft leather, thus apply the compresses, of good size. But if you ever wish to make this plaster more effective for thick sinews and muscles, and hard breasts, and swollen joints, take a pound of the roots of wild mallow, clean and fresh, and cutting them into pieces of the length and thickness of a finger, throw the pieces into what is boiling in the pot. And when, having been charred (ἀνθρακωθέντ’), they have given up all their moisture and their power to the drug, remove the little roots of the mallow with a strainer, and having sprinkled on the litharge finely ground, then boil, as I said before, and again store it away likewise and use it in the same way.

When the drug, boiled for a moderate time, takes on the thickness of a plaster, and likewise takes on the foul smell of a drug, lift the pot quickly from the fire, making it a little harder in summer, but soft in winter, yet nevertheless non-soiling; and stir it likewise until it has cooled moderately; 1000 then again with rather firm hands sprinkle (?) the whole, once softened, and in this way transfer it from the pot. When there is need, soften it again vigorously, and, having spread it carefully on soft leather, in this way apply the pads of good size. But if you wish at some time to make this plaster more effective for thick sinews and muscles, and hard breasts, and swollen joints, put in a litra of the roots of wild mallow, clean and fresh, and, having cut them into finger-sized pieces, in both length and thickness, throw the pieces onto the contents boiling in the pot. And when, being charred, they have given up all their moisture and potency to the drug, lift out the little roots of the mallow with a strainer, and, having sprinkled on the litharge ground smooth, then boil it, as I said before, and again store it away likewise and use it in the same manner.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἑκτικὸς, Διεθεῖσα
1001 This drug, says Menecrates, the man who first discovered it—a most excellent man, steadfastly devoted to the art—possesses the virtues of both. For it keeps freshly inflicted wounds free of inflammation, and he has persuaded us that it glues together divided parts and treats ulcers that are excessively inflamed, as well as wounded muscles, without any other of the things able to give help—poultices, sponges and fomentations. It does everything that I said earlier the plaster made of litharge and axoungion does. He says that it is also the best pessary in long-standing and in fresh inflammations of the womb, and that it fills up hollow ulcers and, when diluted with rose-oil or at least with good myrtle-oil, brings them to a scar. And in general he says that every poultice and drug applied from outside upon these is superfluous for those who use this one.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἑκτικὸς, Ἔπεισεν ἡμᾶς, Διεθεῖσα, ἐπ’ αὐτῶν
1001 This one, says Menecrates, who first discovered the drug, possesses the virtues of both; he was a most excellent man, of settled proficiency in the art. For it keeps freshly inflicted wounds free of inflammation, and it has persuaded us that it agglutinates what has been divided and treats ulcers that are excessively inflamed, and wounded muscles, without any other of the cataplasms, sponges and embrocations able to give help. It does everything that I said earlier the one made with litharge and axungia does. He says it is best as a pessary in chronic and in fresh inflammations of the uterus, and for filling hollow ulcers, and, dissolved in rose unguent or in good myrtle unguent, for cicatrizing. And in general, he says, every cataplasm and drug applied over them from outside is superfluous for those who use this one.

1002 The drug is worthy of being recorded; and by now it is trusted even by the envious. He says to make it in this manner: seven litrai by weight of oil in summer. In another copy I found: half of the oil, of rich and fresh axoungion. But in winter, as he says, you will put in nine, and only six of most finely ground litharge. Of fenugreek he said a half part of a xestes, and the same of burnt linseed. And of wild mallow he said three litrai by weight, of the root still having its fresh juice, to boil in water, eight xestai by measure, in a new, wide-mouthed earthenware pan, throwing in the root of the mallow cleaned, cut up, fine and bruised, the fenugreek washed, and the linseed likewise well.

1002 The drug is worthy of being recorded. It is by now trusted even by the envious, and he says to make it in this way. Of oil, in summer, seven litrae by weight. (In another copy I found half of the oil [replaced by] rich and fresh axungia.) In winter, as he says, you will put in nine, but only six of very finely ground litharge. Of fenugreek, he said, half a sextarius, and the same of burnt linseed. Of wild mallow he said three litrae by weight, of the root still holding its fresh juice. Boil it in water, eight sextarii by measure, in a new, wide-mouthed earthenware pan, putting in the mallow root, cleaned, cut up, fine and bruised, the washed fenugreek, and the linseed, carefully.

For it works, to be sure, for the conditions I mentioned before, quite simply all of them: the crude ones, those difficult of concoction and resistant to change, 1004 the great wounds in muscles and membranes. For it readily resolves their scars; and applied to all arthritic sufferers, it will soothe the places that have become chronic. It contains: of the driest pitch, two pounds; of pure Pontic wax, two pounds; again, of terebinth, the very moist kind, two pounds; of manna of unadulterated frankincense, one pound; and half a pound, or something a little less, of white, above all fatty, fresh butter. When it has thus cooled and been softened, store it away. 1005 And when you have mixed into this preparation a large quantity of fresh rose-oil, you will use it also for ulcers that are excessively hollow and fleshless, and for inflammations of tendons, and for those conditions which are hard to bring over into white pus. [On akopa.] The professed virtue of the akopa drugs is indicated by their very name. For people call kopoi (fatigues) the conditions that arise in bodies from many or vehement movements, either in the whole body or in those parts that have laboured more. These conditions are painful, most of all to those who are moving, but already also to those who are at rest, in the case of those that are more vehement.

ὕστερον δὲ καὶ τοῖς ἰατροῖς εἰθίσθη, κᾂν μὴν πρὸς κόπους ᾖ τὸ φάρμακον ὑπ’ αὐτῶν συγκείμενον, ἀλλ’ ἤτοι πρὸς ὀδύνην χρονίζουσαν ἐν τῷ βάθει τοῦ σώματος ἢ δυσκινησίαν ἢ δυσαισθησίαν ἢ σκληρότητα μορίου ἢ τάσιν ἢ ὄγκον σκιῤῥώδη καλεῖν ὁμοίως, ἄκοπα καὶ τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα, μόνον ἐὰν ἔχῃ τὴν καθ’ ὑγρότητα σύστασιν ὁμοίαν τοῖς ἀκόποις, ἥτις ἐστὶ παραπλησία 1006 ταῖς ὑγραῖς κηρωταῖς, ὁποίαις κᾀπὶ τῶν ἐξαρθρημάτων τε καὶ καταγμάτων χρώμεθα. τῆς γὰρ τοιαύτης συστάσεως ὑγρότατον μέν ἐστι τὸ καλούμενον ὑπὸ τῶν νεωτέρων ἰατρῶν κηρέλαιον. ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐτοῦ τὰ ἄκοπα χρίσματα καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα παχυτέραν ἔχουσα σύστασιν ἡ τῶν ὑγρῶν κηρωτὴ, εἶθ’ ἡ τῶν ἁπαλῶν τε καὶ μαλακῶν ὀνομαζομένων. ὑστάτη δὲ ἡ τῶν ἀμολύντων, ὁμοία τῇ τῶν καλουμένων ἰδίως ἐπιθεμάτων· οἷς ἐφεξῆς ἐστιν ἡ τῶν ἐμπλάστρων σύστασις, ἐχουσῶν καὶ τούτων αὐτῶν, ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον οὐ μικρὰν διαφορὰν, ὥστ’ ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν, κηρωτομαλάγματά τινα προσηγόρευσαν, ἀπὸ τῆς συστάσεως μόνης οὕτως ὀνομάσαντες, ὅσα τῶν ἐπιτιθεμένων φαρμάκων οὐκέτι μέν ἐστιν ὑγρὰ, σκληρὰ δὲ εἰς τοσοῦτον οὐδέπω, καθάπερ αἱ ἔμπλαστροι. οὐσῶν οὖν ἁπασῶν τῶν εἰρημένων διαφορῶν δηλωτικῶν οὐ τῆς δυνάμεως τῶν φαρμάκων, ἀλλὰ τῆς κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν ἰδέας· τῶν ἀκόπων προσηγορία, πάλαι μὲν ἐσήμανε τὴν δύναμιν τῶν φαρμάκων, ὕστερον δὲ τὴν σύστασιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κᾂν μὴν
Later it became customary also for physicians, even if the drug compounded by them is not for fatigues (kopoi) but rather for a pain lingering in the depth of the body, or for difficulty of movement, or impaired sensation, or hardness of a part, or tension, or a scirrhous swelling, to call all such drugs likewise akopa, provided only that they have a consistency, in respect of fluidity, similar to that of the akopa, which is close to 1006 the moist cerates, such as we use also for dislocations and fractures. For of this kind of consistency the most fluid is what is called by the more recent physicians kerelaion (wax-oil); next after it come the akopa unguents, and after these, having a thicker consistency, the cerate of the moist kind, then that of the ones termed tender and soft. Last is that of the non-staining ones (amolynta), similar to that of what are properly called epithemata; and next after these is the consistency of the plasters, these too having among themselves no small difference in the more and the less, so that some physicians have given the name kerotomalagmata to certain of them, naming them thus from the consistency alone, namely those of the drugs applied externally which are no longer fluid, yet not yet hard to such a degree as the plasters. Since, then, all the differences mentioned are indicative not of the power of the drugs but of their form in respect of consistency, the appellation of akopa formerly signified the power of the drugs, but later their consistency.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κᾂν μὴν (read as κἂν μή), ἀμολύντων, κηρωτομαλάγματα, κηρέλαιον
Later, however, it also became customary among physicians, even if the drug they had compounded was not for fatigues but for a pain lingering chronically in the depth of the body, or for difficulty of movement, or impaired sensation, or hardness of a part, or tension, or a scirrhous swelling, to call all such things likewise akopa (anti-fatigue remedies), provided only that it has a consistency, in respect of fluidity, similar to the akopa; this consistency is close 1006 to the liquid cerates, such as we use also for dislocations and fractures. For of such a consistency the most liquid is what the more recent physicians call kerelaion (wax-oil). Next after it come the akopa ointments, and after these, having a thicker consistency, that of the liquid cerates, then that of the cerates named tender and soft. Last is that of the amolynta (non-soiling ones), similar to that of the things specifically called epithemata (applications). Next after these is the consistency of plasters, and these too have no small difference among themselves in degree, more and less, so that some physicians gave the name kerotomalagmata (cerate-emollients) to certain preparations, naming them thus from their consistency alone: namely those of the applied drugs which are no longer liquid, yet not yet hard to the same degree as plasters. Since, then, all the differences mentioned indicate not the power of the drugs but the form of their consistency, the name of the akopa formerly signified the power of the drugs, but later their consistency.

It is quite clear, then, that the drugs called akopa among the ancients must all be liquid in their consistency, since oil alone treats the superficial fatigues (kopoi), while those that are both deep-seated and more violent it treats together with one of the relaxing drugs thrown into it, which are of a nature to heat moderately, such as galbanum and terebinth resin 1008 and, in addition to it, a certain resin called colophonian and the roasted (phrykte). Hotter than these are fir resin and strobilos (pine-cone) resin, while larch resin is slightly hotter than terebinth. All such things are mixed with relaxing oils, of the kind that Sabine oil is, wax also sometimes being added. An akopon unguent for those already in pain deep down is also the one made from the seed of the fir, chamomile, dill and black-poplar flower, concerning which we shall say in what follows how it is prepared. The sousinon (lily unguent), the gleukinon (must unguent) and the Commagene unguent are also among the akopon unguents, as are all those compounded with soft fat (pimele) and hard fat (stear), pig's fat relaxing most moderately, hens' fat more than these, and goose fat still more than this. A stronger fat, so as now to heat more violently and for this reason also to disperse considerably, is lion's and leopard's fat. In the middle between these and ox and goat fat is that of foxes and of the hyena.

of cardamom 1010 half a xestes, of savin half a xestes, of sweetish wine three xestai, of Venafran oil three litrai, of wax two litrai. Having put the dry ingredients into the wine, leave them to steep for three days; on the following day, having added the oil and stirred carefully and set it on charcoals, boil, stirring. And when it has boiled up continuously, press out the liquid, and having grated the wax and added it to this and set it again on the fire, boil. And when the wax has melted, having taken it off the fire, let it cool, and having cooled it, store it in a glass or tin vessel and use it, sometimes undiluted, sometimes also diluted with one of the perfumed oils. Another. Take of marjoram one xestes, of libanotis (rosemary) one xestes, of fenugreek one xestes, of bay twigs six litrai, of flower of schoinos (rush) three ounces, of old wine three xestai, of sweet oil three xestai, of the root of wild cucumber and of Celtic nard half a litra each, of wax three litrai. Prepare and use as has been stated before. Another drug, a well-proven one. It is effective also for the paretic, for the tremulous, and for every nervous sympathetic affection. Menius Rufus indeed used it.

of cardamom 1010 half a sextarius, of savin half a sextarius, of sweetish wine 3 sextarii, of Venafran oil 3 pounds, of wax 2 pounds. Put the dry ingredients into the wine and leave them to steep for 3 days; on the following day put in the oil, stir carefully, set it over coals, and boil it while stirring. When it has boiled up repeatedly, press out the liquid; grate the wax, throw it into this, set it on the fire again, and boil. When the wax has melted, take it off the fire and leave it to cool; once it has cooled, store it in a vessel of glass or of tin and use it, sometimes unmixed, sometimes diluted with one of the perfumed oils. Another. Menius Rufus indeed used it.

It works for sufferers from podagra, from arthritis and from sciatica, 1011 so as to deliver them from the whole condition. Its use is in the intervals; during the paroxysms we use the drug diluted. ♃ Of marjoram 2 xestai, of fenugreek 1 xestes, of libanotis 1½ xestai, of melilot 1 xestes, of Illyrian (iris) 1 litra, of cyperus 1 litra, of savin 1 litra, of cachrys 1 litra, of cardamom half a litra, of flower of schoinos half a litra, of cassia half a litra, of southernwood half a litra, of myrtle fruit half a litra, of bay fruit half a litra. Having bruised all of these and put them into a double perfumer's vessel, add 8 xestai of Tmolite wine; let them steep for 3 days; then add 16 litrai of Sabine oil, 1 litra of iris-unguent, 1 litra of mastic-unguent, 1 litra of Syrian, 1 litra of gleukinon, 1 litra of myrtle-unguent, 1 litra of bay-unguent, and setting it on the fire boil it; then, having pressed out the liquid from the boiled ingredients, pour it off into another vessel and boil again, apart from the boiled-out materials, adding half a litra of wax, 1 litra of larch resin, of myrrh (and) of stacte 1 litra each; and when these have melted, use as has been said before. Another. ♃ Of marjoram 2 litrai, of fenugreek 2 xestai, of libanotis 2 xestai.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κυνικῶς σπωμένοις, τετράμυρον
The acopon inscribed as 'the one through honey' 1013. It is effective for fatigue-like discomforts and for contractions of the nerves. It is effective for those suffering opisthotonus and for those convulsed in the canine manner; it is called tetramyron (the four-unguent). ♃ Honey, 3 ounces; iris oil, 3 ounces; rose-oil, 3 ounces; henna oil, 3 ounces; must-oil, 3 ounces; wax, 3 ounces; terebinth, 3 ounces. Boil the honey separately. Melt the meltable ingredients and, having put them into a mortar, beat them all up together, and having ground fine, take it up and use. Another. ♃ Henna oil, iris oil, rose-oil, must-oil, 6 kotylai of each; wax, 24 drachms; terebinth, 8 drachms; honey, 6 drachms. Prepare and use as has been stated before. Another, which is called lexopyreton (fever-ceasing). ♃ Rose-oil, 4 kotylai; iris oil, 4; must-oil, 4; henna oil, 4 kotylai; wax, 12 drachms; terebinth, 14 drachms; stacte myrrh, 4 drachms; boiled honey, 16 drachms. The akopon called 'the one with honey' 1013. It is effective against fatigue-like discomforts and contractions of the nerves. It is effective for opisthotonics and for those convulsed in the canine manner. It is called tetramyron. Take: honey 3 oz.; iris oil 3 oz.; rose-oil 3 oz.; oil of cypros 3 oz.; must-oil 3 oz.; wax 3 oz.; terebinth 3 oz. Boil the honey separately. Melt the meltable ingredients, put them into a mortar and pound all together; having ground them fine, take the mixture up and use it. Another. Take: oil of cypros, iris oil, rose-oil, must-oil, 6 kotylai of each; wax 24 dr.; terebinth 8 dr.; honey 6 dr. Prepare and use as described above. Another, called lexopyreton. Take: rose-oil 4 kotylai; iris oil 4; must-oil 4; oil of cypros 4 kotylai; wax 12 dr.; terebinth 14 dr.; percolated myrrh 4 dr.; boiled honey 16 dr. Prepare as described above. Another, from the remedies of Aphrodiseus. Take: rose-oil 4 kotylai; iris oil 4 kotylai; oil of cypros 4 kotylai; wax 12 dr.; must-oil 4 kotylai; goose fat 12 dr.; terebinth 4 dr.; stakte 4 dr.; opobalsam 2 dr.; boiled honey 8 dr. Prepare in the usual way.

Another, for chronic conditions, 1017 exceedingly noble. Take: wax, 1 mina; old oil, 4 xestai; terebinth resin, 1 litra; myrrh, 4 ounces; frankincense, opopanax, root of wild cucumber, verdigris, nitron, 4 ounces each; white pepper, 2 ounces; galbanum, 4 ounces; deer marrow, 4 ounces; henna oil, 4 ounces; iris oil, 4 ounces; quince oil, 4 ounces; of the shoots of green rue, 3 ounces; vinegar, a sufficient quantity. Boil the roots of the wild cucumber and the rue with the vinegar, then press out the liquid and add it to the dry ingredients, and having triturated carefully, take it up with the meltable ingredients, and use the drug sometimes undiluted, and at times also diluted. This is done in proportion to the underlying conditions. Another, a successful drug of Bassus. He himself, having used this, after having previously been paralysed, was saved. Take: myrtle oil, 4 ounces; henna oil, 4 ounces; old oil, 1½ litrai; chalkanthos, 1½ litrai; wax, 1 litra; terebinth resin, 1½ litrai; galbanum, 3 ounces; manna, 4 ounces; opopanax, 2 ounces; bryony root, 4 ounces; verdigris, 4 ounces; aphronitron, 4 ounces.

ταύτῃ τῇ σκευασίᾳ χρηστέον ἐπὶ τῇ Σαβερνίῳ Οὐαλέντι σκευασθείσῃ ποδάρκῃ, ἥτις ἐπιγράφεται 1022 Πομπηίου πολυτελής. τὸ διὰ τῆς αἰγείρου, ἰσχιαδικοῖς, ἀρθριτικοῖς, παρετικοῖς, τρομώδεσι, ποιεῖ καὶ πρὸς χρῆσιν καθημερινήν. ♃ αἰγείρου ἀκρεμόνων μνᾶν α΄. σικύου ἀγρίου ῥίζης λίτρας δ΄. κασσίας σύριγγος λίτρας β΄. μύρου κροκίνου λίτρας β΄. κρόκου γο γ΄. οἴνου εὐώδους ξε. ιβ΄. ἐλαίου Σαβίνου ξε. τ΄. σαμψύχου λίτρας δ΄. κηροῦ λίτρας δ΄. τοὺς ἀκρέμονας τῆς αἰγείρου κόψας ἐπιμελῶς, ἔχε ἐν ἀγγείῳ διπλῷ μυρεψικῷ καὶ τούτοις ἐπίβαλλε σάμψυχον, ῥίζαν σικύου κόψας παχυμερῶς καὶ τούτοις ἐπίβαλλε τὸν οἶνον καὶ πάντα ἔα βρέχεσθαι ἐπὶ ἡμέρας θ΄. τῇ δ’ ἐπιούσῃ ἐπιβαλὼν τὸ ἔλαιον καὶ ἀνακόψας ἐπιμελῶς ἕψε, πυρὶ χρώμενος μαλακῷ. ἔπειτα τὸ ὑγρὸν ἐκθλίψας καὶ τούτῳ ἐπιβαλὼν κηρὸν κατακεκνησμένον καὶ μυελὸν λεανθέντα καὶ θεὶς ἐπὶ τὸ πῦρ ἕψε· ὅταν δὲ τακῇ, ἄρας ἀπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς, ἐπίβαλλε τὸ κρόκινον, κινῶν συνεχῶς. τὸ δὲ κρόκινον λεάνας, ἀναλάμβανε αἵματι χερσαίας χελώνης, ὥστε ἔχειν ἐλαίου παλαιοῦ τὸ πάχος. σφάζεται δὲ ἡ χελώνη κατὰ τὴν κατακλεῖδα καλάμῳ ἀποξανθέντι ἀμφότερα τὰ μέρη.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ποδάρκῃ, ξε. τ΄, κρόκινον, μυελὸν, ἀποξανθέντι ἀμφότερα τὰ μέρη
This preparation is to be used following upon the Podarces prepared for Sabernius Valens, which is entitled 1022 'Pompey's sumptuous'. The one made with black poplar: for sciatics, arthritics, paralytics, the tremulous; it is effective also for daily use. Take: of shoots of black poplar, 1 mina; of root of wild cucumber, 4 litrai; of tube-cassia, 2 litrai; of crocine unguent, 2 litrai; of saffron, 3 ounces; of fragrant wine, 12 xestai; of Sabine oil, 300 xestai; of marjoram, 4 litrai; of wax, 4 litrai. Having chopped the shoots of the poplar carefully, keep them in a double perfumer's vessel, and add to these the marjoram and the cucumber root, chopped coarsely, and to these add the wine, and leave everything to steep for 9 days. On the following day, having added the oil and beaten it up thoroughly, boil, using a gentle fire. Then, having pressed out the liquid and added to it grated wax and triturated marrow, and having set it upon the fire, boil; and when it has melted, taking it off the fire, add the crocine, stirring continuously. The crocine, having triturated it, take up with the blood of a land tortoise, so that it has the thickness of old oil. The tortoise is slaughtered at the collar-bone with a reed sharpened on both sides.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπὶ τῇ ... ποδάρκῃ, ποδάρκῃ, ξε. τ΄, ἀποξανθέντι, τὸ δὲ κρόκινον λεάνας
This preparation is to be used with the podarkes prepared for Sabernius Valens, which is entitled 1022 'the costly one of Pompeius'. The one made with poplar: for sufferers from sciatica, the arthritic, the paralysed, and the tremulous; it also serves for daily use. Take: of poplar shoots, 1 mina; of the root of wild cucumber, 4 pounds; of cassia in pipes, 2 pounds; of saffron unguent, 2 pounds; of saffron, 3 ounces; of fragrant wine, 12 sextarii; of Sabine oil, 300 (?) sextarii; of sampsuchum, 4 pounds; of wax, 4 pounds. Having carefully chopped the poplar shoots, keep them in a double perfumer's vessel, and add to them the sampsuchum and the cucumber root, chopped coarsely; add the wine to these, and let everything steep for 9 days. On the following day, add the oil, beat it up carefully, and boil it, using a gentle fire. Then squeeze out the liquid, add to it wax scraped fine and marrow that has been ground smooth, set it on the fire and boil it; when it has melted, take it off the fire and add the saffron unguent, stirring continuously. Having ground the saffron preparation, take it up with the blood of a land tortoise, so that it has the thickness of old oil. The tortoise is slaughtered at the collarbone with a reed scraped on both sides.

μύρου μαλαβαθρίνου 1024 λίτραν α΄. σαμψύχου λίτραν α΄. τὰς ῥίζας τέμνων εἰς παχέα καὶ βαλὼν σὺν τῷ σαμψύχῳ εἰς τὸ ἔλαιον καὶ θεὶς ἐπ’ ἀνθράκων ἕψε καὶ ὅταν ἀναξηρανθῶσιν αἱ ῥίζαι, ἔκθλιβε τὸ ὑγρὸν καὶ τὰς μὲν ῥίζας καὶ τὸ σάμψυχον ἀπόῥῤιπτε. τοὺς δὲ βατράχους καθεὶς εἰς σπυρίδιον πλεκτὸν καὶ τούτου τὰ χείλη συῤῥάψας καὶ θεὶς εἰς τὸ ἐκθλιβὲν ἔλαιον καὶ πάλιν θεὶς τὸ ἀγγεῖον ἐπὶ τὸ πῦρ, πωμαζόμενον. ἱκανοῦ δὲ διαστήματος γενομένου, ὥστε τοὺς βατράχους ἀποτακῆναι, τὸ σπυρίδιον βαστάσας καὶ τὸ ὑγρὸν ἐκθλίψας ῥῖπτε τὸ σπυρίδιον. τῷ δ’ ὑπολειφθέντι ἐλαίῳ ἐπίβαλλε τὰ τηκτὰ κινῶν συνεχῶς. ἔσχατον ἐπίβαλλε μυελὸν καὶ χαλβάνην καὶ ὅταν διαλυθῇ ἄρας ἀπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς καὶ ποσῶς ἐάσας ψυγῆναι κατάπασσε τὰ ξηρὰ κοπέντα καὶ σησθέντα λεπτοτάτῳ κοσκίνῳ κινῶν συνεχῶς καὶ εἰς θυείαν ἐξεράσας καὶ ἀνακόψας, ἐπίβαλλε μαλαβαθρίνου μύρου λίτραν α΄ S". καὶ ἀνακόψας, ἀνελόμενος εἰς ἀγγεῖον μολυβδοῦν, ἀδιάπνευστον φύλαττε. χρηστέον δὲ τῷ φαρμάκῳ, ὅτε μὲν ὡς μαλάγματι παρακμῆς γενομένης. ἔστι δ’ ὅτε καὶ συγχρίσματι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συγχρίσματι, παρακμῆς
of malabathrum unguent 1024 one pound; of marjoram one pound. Cutting the roots into thick pieces and throwing them together with the marjoram into the oil, and setting it over charcoals, boil; and when the roots have dried out, press out the liquid, and throw away the roots and the marjoram. Then having put the frogs into a small woven basket and sewn up its lips, and having placed it in the oil that was pressed out, and having again set the vessel upon the fire, covered with a lid. When a sufficient interval has passed, so that the frogs have melted away, lift out the small basket and, having pressed out the liquid, throw the basket away. To the oil that is left over add the fusible ingredients, stirring continuously. Last of all add the marrow and the galbanum, and when it has dissolved, taking it off the fire and allowing it to cool somewhat, sprinkle on the dry ingredients, pounded and sifted through the finest sieve, stirring continuously; and having poured it out into a mortar and beaten it up, add one pound and a half of malabathrum unguent, and having beaten it up, take it up into a leaden vessel and keep it so that it cannot evaporate. The drug is to be used at one time as a malagma, when the decline (of the affection) has set in; and there are times when it is also used as a liniment.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πωμαζόμενον, ποσῶς, S", παρακμῆς, συγχρίσματι, χαλβάνην (galbanum is not in the ingredient list given)
Of malabathrum unguent, 1024 1 pound; of sampsuchum, 1 pound. Cutting the roots into thick pieces, and putting them together with the sampsuchum into the oil, and setting it over coals, boil; and when the roots have dried out, squeeze out the liquid, and throw away the roots and the sampsuchum. Then, letting the frogs down into a little woven basket, sewing up its lips, and putting it into the squeezed-out oil, set the vessel back on the fire, covered with a lid. When a sufficient interval has passed for the frogs to have melted away, lift out the basket, squeeze out the liquid, and throw the basket away. To the oil that remains add the meltable ingredients, stirring continually. Last of all add the marrow and the galbanum; and when it has dissolved, lift it from the fire and let it cool somewhat, then sprinkle on the dry ingredients, pounded and sifted through a very fine sieve, stirring continually; and having emptied it into a mortar and pounded it, add 1½ pounds of malabathrum unguent. Pound again, take it up into a leaden vessel, and keep it airtight. The drug is to be used sometimes as a malagma (emollient), once the abatement of the disease has set in, and sometimes also as an ointment for rubbing on.

1025 λαβόντες γὰρ τοῦ φαρμάκου ὅσον ἐξαρκεῖ, διαλύσαντες μύρῳ καὶ ποιήσαντες κηρωτῆς ὑγρᾶς τὸ πάχος χρώμεθα. ἄλλο τὸ τοῦ Ἁλιέως, φάρμακον ἐπιτετευγμένον, ἐν ᾧ Ἁλιεὺς διατρίβων καὶ ὑπὸ τοῦ πάθους συνεχόμενος ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον, ὥστε τὸ σωμάτιον διαστραφῆναι, ἐχρήσατο τῷ φαρμάκῳ καὶ τῆς ὅλης ἀπηλλάγη διαθέσεως. ἐδόθη ἡ σκευασία, Οὐαλερίῳ Παυλίνῳ. ἔχει δὲ οὕτως. ♃ ἐλαίου Σαβίνου λίτρας γ΄. αἰγείρου ἀκρεμόνων λίτραν α΄. μὴ παρόντων τῶν ἀκρεμόνων, χρηστέον σαμψύχῳ, ὥστε εἶναι τῆς σαμψύχου διπλοῦν. ἔχει δὲ καὶ σαμψύχου λίτραν α΄. κυπρίνου λίτραν α΄. ἰρίνου λίτραν α΄. μαστίχης λίτραν α΄. σμύρνης στακτῆς ἡμίλιτρον, κηροῦ λίτρας γ΄. ἀποχύματος λίτρας γ΄. λίπους ὑείου λιπαροῦ λίτρας γ΄. θείου ἀπύρου γο γ΄. νίτρου ἀφροῦ γο γ΄. χαλβάνης λίτρας γ΄. μυελοῦ ἐλαφείου γο δ΄. χελωνῶν χερσαίων ι΄. τὸ αἷμα ἐμβάλλειν χρὴ, σκευασθέντι τῷ φαρμάκῳ καὶ ἤδη τριβομένῳ. σκευάζειν δὲ δεῖ, καθάπερ εἴρηται. ἐν δὲ τῇ χρήσει τοῦ φαρμάκου, αἴροντας χρὴ λίτραν α΄. τούτῳ ἐπιβάλλειν ἡδύσματος χάριν ὀποβαλσάμου γο γ΄.

1025 For taking as much of the drug as suffices, dissolving it in perfume and bringing it to the consistency of a liquid cerate, we use it. Another, that of Halieus, a successful drug, in which Halieus, passing his time and being gripped by the affection to such a degree that his body was distorted, used the drug and was freed from the whole condition. The preparation was given to Valerius Paulinus. It is as follows. Take of Sabine oil three litrai; of young shoots of black poplar one litra; if the shoots are not available, one must use marjoram, so that the marjoram is double. It contains also of marjoram one litra; of henna oil one litra; of iris oil one litra; of mastic one litra; of stacte of myrrh half a litra; of wax three litrai; of apochyma three litrai; of fatty pig's lard three litrai; of unburnt sulphur three ounces; of foam of nitron three ounces; of galbanum three litrai; of deer marrow four ounces; of land tortoises, ten: the blood must be put in when the drug has been prepared and is already being triturated. One must prepare it as has been stated. In the use of the drug, one must take up one litra and add to it, for the sake of fragrance, three ounces of opobalsam

1025 For taking as much of the drug as suffices, dissolving it in unguent and bringing it to the thickness of a liquid cerate, we use it. Another, that of Halieus, a successful drug, in connection with which Halieus, while spending his time [there] and gripped by the affection to such a degree that his poor body was distorted, used the drug and was freed from the entire condition. The preparation was given to Valerius Paulinus. It is as follows. ℞ Of Sabine oil, 3 litrai. Of poplar shoots, 1 litra. Of mastic, 1 litra. Of percolated myrrh, a half-litra. Of wax, 3 litrai. Of apochyma, 3 litrai. Of rich swine's fat, 3 litrai. Of unfired sulphur, 3 ounces. Of foam of nitron, 3 ounces. We divide the oil, and in one part we boil the frogs, in the other the roots; then, having strained the 1027 liquid, we use it for the preparation of the drug, and we use the drug as an akopon. This drug [we prepared] for Valerius Paulinus in this manner, adding to the recipe: of marjoram one litra and a half, of saffron unguent one litra, of malabathron unguent one litra, of opobalsam three ounces, the blood of ten tortoises, of deer marrow two litrai, of galbanum two litrai, of old pig's fat three litrai, of apochyma one litra, of wax three litrai and a half, of unburnt sulphur one ounce, of aphronitron four ounces, three toads, the blood of fifteen tortoises, and in place of the saffron unguent, of Syrian unguent one litra, of marjoram one litra and a half. Prepare in the usual manner. Another, of Pompeius Sabinus; it is entitled "costly." It was prepared for Aburnius Valens; it is effective for those suffering from sciatica, for arthritics, for those with podagra, for those with tremor, and for every nervous affection. The details of the preparation are as follows: ♃ of marjoram two litrai, of fenugreek two litrai, of melilot one litra, of libanotis one litra, of savin one litra and a half, of cachrys one litra, of henna (kyprinon) one litra, of southernwood one litra, of cardamom half a litra, of bay-berries half a litra, of fragrant wine twenty-four xestai.

We divide the oil, and in one part we boil the frogs, in the other the roots; then, having strained the 1027 liquid, we use it for the preparation of the drug, and we use the drug as an acopon. This drug [was made] for Valerius Paulinus, with additions made to the recipe in this way: sampsuchum, 1½ litrae; saffron unguent, 1 litra; malabathrum [unguent], 1 litra; opobalsam, 3 ounces; the blood of 10 tortoises; deer marrow, 2 litrae; galbanum, 2 litrae; old pig fat, 3 litrae; apochyma, 1 litra; wax, 3½ litrae; unfired sulphur, 1 ounce; aphronitre, 4 ounces; toads, 3; the blood of 15 tortoises; and instead of the saffron [unguent], Syrian [unguent], 1 litra; sampsuchum, 1½ litrae. Prepare in the proper manner. Another, of Pompeius Sabinus; it is entitled 'the costly'. It was prepared for Aburnius Valens; it is effective for sciatics, arthritics, the gouty, the tremulous, and for every affection of the sinews. The particulars of its preparation are as follows. Take: sampsuchum, 2 litrae; fenugreek, 2 litrae; melilot, 1 litra; libanotis, 1 litra; savin, 1½ litrae; cachrys, 1 litra; cyprinum, 1 litra; southernwood, 1 litra; cardamom, a half-litra; laurel berries, a half-litra; fragrant wine, 24 sextarii.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνασκεύαζε, ἀνειμένῳ
having taken off as much of the wine as suffices, with this we soak ammoniac incense, frankincense, galbanum, opopanax and the remaining juices; once these have dissolved we triturate them, and to these, once triturated, we add, pounded and sifted, alum, red nitron, colocynth, flower of Asian stone, pepper, and we triturate the rest of the dry ingredients; to these we add 1029 stacte of myrrh and the honey. When these have been prepared in this way, we add all the liquids to the ingredients soaking in the wine and, setting them on the fire, we boil, stirring continuously. And when the greater part of the wine has been consumed, we press out the liquid and, putting the frogs into it, as has been stated above, we boil, and then we add the meltable ingredients; and when these have melted, lifting it from the fire, we add it to the triturated ingredients and, having beaten it up and taken it up, we use it. Another metasyncritic acopon: for sciatics, arthritics, the paralysed, those with tremors. Julius Secundus used it. It removes (ἀνασκεύαζε) chronic conditions; it is good also for those with headache. Take adarkē 2 oz., stacte of myrrh 2 oz., aphronitron 2 oz., euphorbium, opobalsamum 2 ounces each, storax unguent 1 pound. Pound and sift the dry ingredients, and add to these stacte of myrrh and opobalsamum, and having beaten it up, take it up with the storax unguent and use the drug, sometimes undiluted, sometimes slackened; and it assists concoction. Another, composed for Orfitus.

On the fourth day, taking up as much of the wine as is sufficient, we steep in it the ammoniac incense, frankincense, galbanum, opopanax and the remaining juices; when these have dissolved we triturate them; onto these, once triturated, we add, pounded and sifted, the alum, red nitre, colocynth, flower of Assian stone and pepper, and we triturate the rest of the dry ingredients; onto these we add 1029 percolated myrrh and the honey. When these have been prepared in this way, we add all the liquids to the things steeping in the wine, and, setting it on the fire, we boil it, stirring continually. And when most of the wine has been consumed, we squeeze out the liquid, and, putting the frogs into it, as was stated above, we boil it, and then we add the meltable ingredients; and when these have melted, taking it off the fire, we add it to the triturated ingredients, and after pounding it up we take it up and use it. Another acopon, metasyncritic: for sciatics, arthritics, paralytics, those with tremors. Julius Secundus used it. It breaks up chronic conditions; it is effective also for those suffering from headache. ℞ Adarce 2 oz.; percolated myrrh 2 oz.; aphronitre 2 oz.; euphorbium, opobalsam, 2 ounces each; storax unguent 1 litra. Pound and sift the dry ingredients and add to them the percolated myrrh and the opobalsam, and after pounding them up take them up in the storax unguent and use it, sometimes with the drug undiluted, sometimes diluted; it also assists concoction. Another, composed for Orfitus.

of Troglodytic myrrh half a litra, 1031 of euphorbium, adarkē, nitre, half a litra each, of Falernian wine 16 xestai, of malabathrine unguent of the first preparation 2 litrai, of nard of the first composition 1 litra, of foliatum of the first grade 1 litra, of opobalsam 1 litra, of crocinum 1 litra, of styracinum of the first grade 1 litra, of amygdalinum 1 litra, of Commagenian 1 litra, of gleucinum 1 litra, of irinum 1 litra, of hedychrous 2 litrai, of amaracinum of the first grade 2 litrai. Boil in the manner of perfumers. The drug is exceedingly good; it was prepared for Celer the primipilarius. It is effective for sciatics, arthritics, paralytics, those with tremors, for sprains, ruptures, and for the scirrhous conditions of the viscera, and it assists concoction when smeared on the mouth of the stomach and on the back. Another, composed for Aquilia Secundilla. Take euphorbium, opobalsam, stacte of myrrh, 2 unciae each, of foam of nitre 3 unciae, of wax 1 uncia, of malabathrine unguent 1 litra, of foliatum half a litra, of crocinum half a litra, of nard unguent half a litra, of hedychrous half a litra, of amaracinum half a litra; prepare in the usual way. The recipes written above would indeed have sufficed.

of Troglodytic myrrh a half-litra, 1031 of euphorbium, adarce and nitron a half-litra each, of Falernian wine 16 xestai, of malabathrum unguent of the first preparation 2 litrai, of nard of the first composition 1 litra, of foliatum of the first quality 1 litra, of opobalsam 1 litra, of saffron unguent 1 litra, of storax unguent of the first quality 1 litra, of almond unguent 1 litra, of Commagenian 1 litra, of must unguent 1 litra, of iris unguent 1 litra, of hedychroum 2 litrai, of marjoram unguent of the first quality 2 litrai. Boil in the manner of the unguent-makers. The drug is exceedingly good; it was prepared for Celer the primipilarius. It is effective for sufferers from sciatica, for arthritics, for paralytics, for those with tremor, for spasms and ruptures, and for the scirrhous conditions of the viscera, and it assists concoction when anointed on the mouth of the stomach and on the upper back. Another, composed for Aquilia Secundilla. Prepare in the usual way. The foregoing would indeed have sufficed.

For Asclepiades wrote carefully both their preparations 1032 and their uses, whereas Andromachus did so more carelessly, as is his habit, not even indicating what each of them is naturally fitted to do, but leaving that to us; for he has always composed his writings as if for practitioners of the art. But since some of the acopa, though similar to one another, differ in their proportions, for this reason I have written these too in advance, since readers are able, on the basis of what Asclepiades taught, to reason about these as well — which of the acopa will best suit which affections. And I too shall speak, after going through some general accounts concerning them, from which those who attend to what will be said will arrive more methodically at their uses. Take terebinth resin 56 drachms, wax 28 drachms, oesypum 6 drachms, honey 6 drachms, deer marrow 6 drachms, ammoniac incense 6 drachms, galbanum 6 drachms, aphronitron 6 drachms, sometimes opopanax 6 drachms, bay berries 6 drachms, old oil 1 sextarius, wine 2 cotylae; some add castoreum 3 drachms. Another, the one with marjoram. Take wax 1 pound, omphacine oil 3 sextarii, marjoram 1 sextarius, fenugreek 1 sextarius, old wine 4 sextarii, a bundle of mallow, a bundle of linozostis (mercury-plant), roots of wild cucumber. Another, as Antimachus gives it. Take fresh marjoram, libanotis (rosemary), aspalathus, savin — which is herba Sabina — and fenugreek, 2 sextarii of each, wax 1 pound, old wine 3 sextarii, gleucine oil 3 sextarii. Another, from Philocles.

Take: of oil 1035 boiled down for a long time with roots of wild cucumber, 3 xestai; of wax, one mina; of terebinth resin, 1 mina; of deer marrow, 25 drachms; of goose fat, 25 drachms; of galbanum, 12½ drachms; of marjoram, 1 xestes; of rosemary (libanotis), 1 xestes. These last are ground fine with fragrant wine and mixed in; and it becomes a malagma if more wax is mixed in. Another successful akopon, according to Aphrodas. Take: of aphronitron, 3 drachms; of pepper, 40 grains, and the same amount of long pepper; of frankincense, 16 drachms. Having pounded these, pour on 1 kotyle of fragrant Chian or Rhodian wine, 9 drachms of honey; then melt in a dish 12 drachms of Tyrrhenian wax, 42 drachms of bull's fat. Another, which was Asphalartesios'. Take: of krokomagma, 4 drachms; of deer marrow, 12 drachms; of oesypum, 6 drachms; of goose or pig fat, 12 drachms; of terebinth resin, 12 drachms; of iris oil, 18 drachms; of henna oil and of gleukinon oil, 18 drachms each. Melt the honey, then mix it with the dry ingredients, and having ground it together with the things in the mortar, use it. Another akopon, the barbarian one, made with euphorbium, for sufferers from sciatica and for many other things. Take: of opopanax, 1 ounce; of Tyrrhenian wax, 1 ounce; of terebinth resin, 2 ounces.

1041 γλεύκινον δύναμιν ἔχει θερμαντικὴν, ἀνετικήν. ποιεῖ πρὸς τὰς ἐν ὑστέρᾳ διαθέσεις καὶ πρὸς πᾶσαν νευρικὴν συμπάθειαν, τούτου δὲ αἱ σκευασίαι πολλαί. ἡ δὲ λεγομένη ἁπλῆ ἔχει οὕτως. ♃ γλεύ κους προσφάτου Μαρωνείας σταφυλῆς μετρητὴν βαλὼν εἰς ἀγγεῖον κασσιτερινὸν ἕψε ἐπ’ ἀνθράκων, καὶ ὅταν τρὶς ἢ τετράκις ἀναζέσῃ, ἐξεράσας εἰς ὀστράκινον μετρητὴν, τίθει ἐν ἡλίῳ καὶ ἐπίβαλλε ἐλαίου λευκοῦ ὀμφακίνου χοᾶς στ΄. σχίνου μνᾶς στ΄. οἱ δὲ μνᾶς S". νάρδου τὸ ἴσον, καλάμου τὸ ἴσον, ἀσπαλάθου τὸ ἴσον, ἐλάτης μνᾶν α΄· κυπέρου μνᾶν α΄. καρδαμώμου μνᾶς η΄. ἀμώμου τὸ ἴσον, μελιλώτου γο ε΄. ἔστω δὲ πάντα λεῖα, ἔπειτα συγκινήσας ἐπὶ ἡμέρας η΄. καὶ τὸ γλεῦκος ἀποκαθάρας χρῶ. γλευκίνου σκευασία, ὡς Διοσκορίδης ἐν τῷ α΄. περὶ ὕλης. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐλάτης, σχίνου, μετρητὴν (ὀστράκινον)
1041 Gleucine has a heating, relaxing power. It is effective against the conditions in the uterus and against every nervous sympathy; and of this there are many preparations. The one called simple is as follows. Take a metretes of fresh must of Maronean grapes, put it into a tin vessel and boil over charcoals, and when it has boiled up three or four times, pour it out into an earthenware metretes-vessel, set it in the sun and add 6 choes of white omphacine oil, 6 minae of lentisk (others: half a mina), of nard the same, of calamus the same, of aspalathus the same, 1 mina of elate, 1 mina of cyperus, 8 minae of cardamom, of amomum the same, 5 ounces of melilot. Let all be finely ground; then, having stirred them together for 8 days and cleared off the must, use it. Preparation of gleucine, as Dioscorides in book 1 of On Materia Medica. Dioscorides wrote thus.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐλάτης, νευρικὴν συμπάθειαν, ἀποκαθάρας, μνᾶς η΄, ἀνετικήν
1041 Gleucinum has a warming, relaxing power. It is effective for affections in the uterus and for every sympathetic affection of the sinews; its preparations are many. The one called simple is as follows. ℞ Put a metretes of fresh must of the Maroneian grape into a tin vessel and boil it over coals; when it has boiled up three or four times, pour it off into an earthenware metretes, set it in the sun, and add white omphacine oil, 6 choes; schinus, 6 minas (others, ½ mina); nard, the same amount; calamus, the same amount; aspalathus, the same amount; elate, 1 mina; cyperus, 1 mina; cardamom, 8 minas; amomum, the same amount; melilot, 5 ounces. Let everything be ground smooth; then, after stirring them together for 8 days and clearing off the must, use it. Preparation of gleucinum, as Dioscorides in the first book On Materia Medica. Dioscorides wrote as follows.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συμπεριτριβομένων, μέσων στεμφύλων, μητρῴας ἀρτεμισίας, σπλάγχνου, ἡδυσάρου
Gleukinon in its simple form is prepared from omphakine oil, mastic (schinos), Celtic nard, calamus, spathe, aspalathos, melilot, kostos and must, the grape-pressings (stemphyla) being rubbed together in the middle (?) in the vessel that holds the aromatics, the wine and 1042 the oil; it is stirred for 30 days, twice a day, then it is pressed out and stored away. It has a warming, emollient, relaxing power, acting against shivering-fits and all nervous conditions and those in the womb. It is more beneficial than any akopon, being emollient. Preparation of gleukinon, according to Heras. But since Heras wrote the preparation of gleukinon more clearly than they did, at the end of his book on drugs, I shall set out his text too, word for word, as follows. Composition of gleukinon oil, which acts against chillings and especially nervous conditions; it benefits those suffering from opisthotonos and those suffering from emprosthotonos, applied warm by means of clean wool soaked in the oil and laid upon the places. ♃ Venafran oil, 208 litrai, otherwise 250; old, fragrant Falernian wine, 3 xestai; Aminaean must, 108 xestai; elecampane (helenion), 12 litrai; spathe, 5 litrai; aspalathos, 5 litrai; hedysaron, 2½ litrai; mother(?) artemisia, 2½ litrai; kyperos, 2 litrai; myrrh, 3 litrai, others 1 litra; white pepper, 2½ litrai; splanchnon, 2½ litrai; xylobalsamon (balsam wood), 2½ litrai.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συμπεριτριβομένων, μέσων, στεμφύλων, ἀνεκτικὴν, μητρῴας ἀρτεμισίας, σπλάγχνου, ἐπὶ τῷ τέλει τῆς φαρμακίτιδος βίβλου
The simple gleucinum is prepared from omphacine oil, mastic (schinus), Celtic nard, calamus, spathe, aspalathus, melilot, costus and must, with grape-pomace (stemphyla) placed in the middle being rubbed round together in the vessel that holds the aromatics, the wine and 1042 the oil. It is stirred for thirty days, twice a day; then it is pressed out and stored. It has a warming, emollient, relaxing power, effective against rigors, against all nervous affections, and against those in the uterus. It is of more benefit than any acopon, being emollient. Preparation of gleucinum, according to Heras. Since Heras wrote out the preparation of gleucinum more clearly than they did, at the end of his pharmaceutical book, I shall set out his text too, word for word, as follows. Composition of gleucine oil, effective against chills and above all against nervous affections; it benefits those with opisthotonos and those with emprosthotonos, applied warm by means of clean wool soaked in the oil and laid on the affected places. ♃ Venafran oil, 208 litrae (otherwise, 250); old fragrant Falernian wine, 3 sextarii; Aminaean must, 108 sextarii; helenium, 12 litrae; spathe, 5 litrae; aspalathus, 5 litrae; hedysarum, 2½ litrae; 'mother' artemisia, 2½ litrae; cyperus, 2 litrae; myrrh, 3 litrae (others, 1 litra); white pepper, 2½ litrae; splanchnon, 2½ litrae; xylobalsamum, 2½ litrae.

of long aristolochia 1043 1 pound 3 ounces; of dry myrtle leaves 1 ounce; of dry libanotis 5 ounces; of dry bay leaves 12 ounces; of bdellium 2½ pounds; of Illyrian iris 2½ pounds; of storax 1 pound 3 ounces; of dry cassia 1 pound 3 ounces; of ladanum 1 pound 3 ounces; of cardamom 1 pound 3 ounces; of saffron 1 pound 3½ ounces; of balsam fruit 2½ pounds; of frankincense 5 pounds; of crocomagma 5 pounds; of panax 1 pound 3 ounces; of aromatic reed 5 pounds; of asarum 2½ pounds; of black cassia 2½ pounds; of amomum cluster 1 pound 3 ounces; of flower of schoinos 5 pounds; of costus 5 pounds 3 ounces; of Celtic nard 5 pounds; of melilot 1 pound 3 ounces; of marjoram 1 pound 3 ounces; of dry roses 1 pound 3 ounces; of fenugreek ½ pound, or 2½ pounds; of opobalsamum 5 small vessels; of malabathrum unguent 1 pound. As for the bdellium, even if it is not dry, pound it too; but if it is oily, dissolve it in the wine, pounding it in a mortar.

Greek (Kühn)
βαλὼν εἰς μικρὸν ἀγγεῖον μυρεψικὸν κασσιτερινὸν, πωμάσας 1044 ἐπιμελῶς ἔα ἐπὶ δώδεκα ἡμέρας καὶ δώδεκα νύκτας. συγχρονισάτω δὲ τὸ γλεῦκος καθαρῶς σταφυλῆς ἐκτεθλιμμένης, ἵνα μηδὲν ἔχῃ δρόσου. βαλὼν δὲ τὸ γλεῦκος εἰς μέγα χαλκεῖον, ὥστε καὶ εἰς τὸ ζέμα τοῦ γλεύκους χώραν ἔχειν, ἐπιβάλλειν πάντα ἐν τῷ οἴνῳ βεβρεγμένα καὶ τὸ ἔλαιον καὶ τὸν οἶνον δὶς τῆς ἡμέρας ἐν σπάθῃ μυρεψικῇ σαλευέσθω ἐπὶ ὥραν. ἡ δὲ σπάθη ἀπομασσέσθω ἐπιμελῶς, ἵνα μὴ τὸ προσμεῖναν ἐν αὐτῇ ὀξῦσαν βλάπτῃ τὸ φάρμακον πάλιν καταθείσης τῆς σπάθης. ἐσκευάσθω δὲ τὸ σκεῦος πώματι καὶ ἄνωθεν κεντρῶσι πλείοσι καὶ περιδείσθω ἐπιμελῶς. ὑποκείσθω δὲ τῷ ἀγγείῳ σπείρα. ὅταν δὲ ἡμέραι ιε΄. διέλθωσι, τὸ ἀμμωνιακὸν καὶ αὐτὸ τὸ ἄμωμον ἀποδιδόσθω, ὁλοσχερέστερον κεκομμένον. λ΄. δὲ ἡμέρας ἐν διπλῷ ἀγγείῳ ἐπ’ ἀνθράκων δύο ἢ τρία ζέματα δεῖ ἐνδιδόναι, τῷ μύρῳ ἀποδιδόντας καὶ τὸ ὀποβάλσαμον. καθελόντας δὲ τὸ ἀγγεῖον καὶ ψύξαντας ἐπὶ ποσὸν ἀποχεῖν τὸ μύρον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀξῦσαν, κεντρῶσι, συγχρονισάτω, δρόσου, σπείρα
Having put it into a small tin perfumer's vessel, and having stoppered it 1044 carefully, leave it for twelve days and twelve nights. And let the must coincide in time, from grapes cleanly pressed out, so that it may have no dew in it. Having put the must into a large copper vessel, so that there is room also for the boiling-up of the must, add all the things that have been soaked in the wine, both the oil and the wine; and twice a day let it be stirred with a perfumer's spathe for an hour. And let the spathe be wiped off carefully, so that what has remained on it, having turned sour (ὀξῦσαν), may not harm the drug when the spathe is put in again. Let the vessel be fitted with a lid, and on top with several patchwork cloths (κεντρῶσι), and let it be bound round carefully. And let a coiled pad be placed under the vessel. When fifteen days have passed, let the ammoniac and the amomum itself be added, pounded rather coarsely. And for thirty days, in a double vessel over charcoals, one must give it two or three boilings, adding to the unguent also the opobalsamum. Then, having taken down the vessel and cooled it to a certain degree, pour off the unguent.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συγχρονισάτω, δρόσου, κεντρῶσι, σπείρα, λ΄. δὲ ἡμέρας
Put it into a small perfumer's vessel made of tin, cover it 1044 carefully with a lid, and leave it for twelve days and twelve nights. Let the must be made ready at the same time, from grapes cleanly pressed out, so that it contains nothing of dew. Then, putting the must into a large bronze cauldron, so that there is room also for the boiling-up of the must, add all the ingredients that have been soaked in the wine, together with the oil and the wine; and let it be stirred twice a day with a perfumer's spatula for an hour. Let the spatula be wiped carefully, so that what has remained on it and turned sour may not spoil the drug when the spatula is put in again. Let the vessel be fitted with a lid and, above, with several piercings (κεντρῶσι), and let it be tied round carefully. Let a coiled ring be placed under the vessel. When fifteen days have passed, let the ammoniac and the amomum itself be added, pounded rather coarsely. At thirty days one must give it two or three boilings in a double vessel over coals, adding the opobalsam to the unguent as well. Then, taking the vessel off and letting it cool for a while, pour off the unguent.

Having pounded coarsely those ingredients that need to be pounded, pour on water and, in turn, the pounded ingredients; pour on in addition also the wine and the oil 1046 and let it stand overnight, and on the following day boil it until these ingredients, floating in it, have been thoroughly boiled down and the oil has taken on the quality from the boiled ingredients. And these must often be applied for similar pains and conditions of ill-affection, 1048 to those who are easily overcome by chills and fatigues, if a person, having used them beforehand, goes out in that state to the services imposed by the necessity of life. And it also puts flesh on certain parts of the body that are wasting, if one anoints the affected parts after food; and it will suit those who recover with difficulty, and old men who use them in winter toward evening. But what each of the akopa does we shall state in other verses, appending the useful information to their recipes, or even placing it before the recipe. Now, however, I shall say how one must make the first. Of ammoniac, then, ten (οὐγγίαι), whether one prefers ounces or another weight; the same of frankincense; of galbanum four; of deer marrow four; and four, the same amount, of the oesypum which they send from Attica; of transparent terebinth four; and of good fresh honey again ten.

and those who must often bear similar pains and hardships, 1048 those easily seized by chills and fatigues, if one, having used them beforehand, then goes out to the labours required by the necessities of life; and it puts flesh on parts of the body that are wasting (ἀτροφοῦντα), if one anoints the affected parts again after food; and it will suit those who are slow to recover, and old men, if they use them in winter towards evening. In a larger mortar, having crushed the frankincense into quite small lumps, and the ammoniac too, pouring on wine at once, and having allowed these to be soaked through in due measure, grind with the pestle and, bringing it to the thickness of honey, add the honey and the oesypum and the deer marrow; then, uniting these in the mortar, melt the wax in a pot together with all the oil and the fat and the galbanum and the terebinth, and having let them all melt, pour them over the things in the mortar, 1050 and having stirred a little with the pestle, so that it may cool somewhat, then rub all these together with the hands until everything is united and takes on the thickness of good liquid honey; and so store it away in a vessel of tin or even of boxwood, using it carefully as a most excellent drug. It is also most beneficial for women in whom there has arisen an inflammation of the womb bringing hardnesses and fear of scirrhus; in their case order that the drug, smeared on a pessary, be thus applied to the womb. This also works for pains of the ears, those without ulceration, the violent ones unaccompanied by a flux: mix a fourth or a third part of Sergetic (Σεργητικοῦ) iris unguent with the drug, and having soaked wool in it while warm, insert it into the passage. 1057 Prepare it thus: put a third part of the malabathrum, together with the wax cut up into the smallest pieces, into a thin silver bowl. Having melted it over very hot water, cool and scrape it, and adding it, ground smooth, in the opobalsamum and the cinnamon, grind again, uniting the whole; then finally, pouring the malabathrum into them, unite it by rubbing with the hands, and so store it in a silver vessel or a thick glass one; for in this way you will keep the drug safely. But if you wish to make it more effective 1058 for the conditions I mentioned before, add four drachms of Atlantic euphorbium. For thus it will be pleasant and active, even though it is composed of few ingredients. But I have come to know some who use many fats, and much marrow, and certain aromatics, and juices and unguents and certain oils; and having in turn, of course, made use of these, I recognised that such things belong to men without taste, who have not judged the ingredients and the simple natures of these.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀμέλει, Ἀπειροκάλων, κεκρικότων, μίγματα
But if you wish to make it more efficacious 1058 for the conditions I mentioned before, add four drachms of Atlantic euphorbium. For in this way it will be pleasant and active, even though it is composed of few ingredients. I have known some, however, who use many fats, and much marrow, and certain aromatics, and juices, and unguents, and certain oils; having in turn made use of these myself, I came, of course, to recognise that such things belong to men lacking in taste, who have not discerned the ingredients and the simple natures of these.
